2. A circular pair (`circ1` ↔ `circ2`) → `400` for installing either; confirmed neither was ever registered (`GET` → `404`), not just that the install call failed.
3. A recipe depending on a name with no `.recipe` file → `400`.
4. Upgrade: `leaf` installed at `1.0`; its recipe rewritten to `2.0` (a genuinely new fixture tarball + checksum, not just a version-string bump) — `GET` shows `available_version: "2.0"` *before* any upgrade call, proving the visibility field is live, not just correct after the fact; a plain re-`POST` → `409`; `{"upgrade": true}` → `202`, polls to `installed` at `2.0`, and the installed binary is actually executed and its real stdout checked against the *new* fixture's expected output — proof the rebuild used the new source, not a stale cache re-labeled with a new version string.

Zero warnings; all 13 suites re-run across 3 consecutive full back-to-back runs with no failures.

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** version-constrained dependencies (any installed version satisfies a dependency, matching most source-based package managers' own v1-era simplicity); concurrent installs (still one at a time — a dependency chain already serializes through that same slot); a bulk "upgrade everything outdated" command (upgrades are per-package and explicit); symlink support in a package's own `DESTDIR` output.

## Phase 11 — Bare-metal boot: kernel, bootloader, A/B root, installer

Every prior phase built and verified `kanxeod` as a guest process running on top of an already-booted Linux system — this dev environment has, since Phase 1, always been a privileged LXC container on someone else's kernel. Phase 11 is what makes Kanxeo the operating system itself: a kernel we build, a bootloader that starts it, and an installer that puts it on a real disk, with `kanxeod` as PID 1.

**Architecture confirmed with the user before any code was written, the same discipline every prior phase used for its own "how does this actually work" questions:**

- **Firmware: UEFI only.** No BIOS/legacy support in v1 — narrows the bootloader choice to something UEFI-native and genuinely thin, and avoids GRUB's hybrid-BIOS+UEFI complexity and its own scripting/config language entirely.
- **Bootloader: `systemd-boot`.** A boot *manager*, not a shell — reads loader entries from the EFI System Partition (ESP) directly, no config language of its own to maintain. Its native Automatic Boot Assessment (a `+LEFT-DONE` suffix convention on the loader entry's filename) is reused as-is for A/B rollback rather than inventing a second boot-counting mechanism — one already-correct, already-tested implementation instead of a bespoke one.
- **Root filesystem: read-only A/B, `squashfs`.** Two identical-role root slots; only one is active at a time. Read-only close the door on the entire class of "a bad write during normal operation corrupts the OS" failure — the actual "in case of corruption" property the user asked for. `squashfs` (compressed, natively read-only, one `CONFIG_SQUASHFS` kernel option, no exotic dependency) makes writing a slot a single atomic whole-image operation: the same code path serves both the installer's initial write and a later update's write to the *inactive* slot, so building the update mechanism costs almost nothing extra once the installer can write an image at all.
- **Root scope: thin, control-plane only.** Root A/B holds exactly the kernel, `kanxeod`, and the handful of real binaries it shells out to (`openssl`, `curl`, `tar`, `sha256sum`, `dnsmasq`, `cp`, `rm`, ...) — never the package manager's canonical base container image or any container's own data. This keeps the two things that actually have independent lifecycles (the OS itself, and installed packages/running containers) on independent storage: a package install never requires an A/B flip, and an OS rollback never reverts installed packages or container state. The alternative (a fatter root carrying the base image too) was explicitly rejected for conflating two lifecycles that don't change together.
- **No initramfs.** The bootloader loads the kernel directly; the kernel mounts the active root slot itself (its storage driver and `squashfs` support built in, not modular) and `exec`s `kanxeod` via `init=`. This is the thinnest possible boot chain and needs nothing built/regenerated on a kernel update beyond the kernel image itself — but it only works because of the next decision:
- **Target hardware: fixed/known, not arbitrary bare metal.** This is not a general-purpose installer meant to boot on any PC — the kernel's defconfig only needs to carry the specific storage and NIC drivers the actual target machine(s) need. This is what makes "no initramfs" safe: a genuinely arbitrary-hardware installer would need dynamic module loading before root is even reachable, which is exactly what an initramfs is for.
- **A/B failover: automatic.** `systemd-boot`'s boot-counter marks a slot "trying" on boot; `kanxeod`, once it reaches a real healthy state (detailed in part 2), renames its own loader entry to mark the boot successful — a plain `rename(2)` on the ESP, not a `bootctl` subprocess call, consistent with this project's hand-rolled-daemon posture (ADR-0007) for something this simple. An unconfirmed boot after the counter's retry budget is exhausted falls back to the other slot with no operator involved.
- **Kernel: built from mainline source**, honoring MISSION.md's literal "the host OS... compiled directly from source," with a minimal defconfig scoped to exactly what the target hardware and this design need (cgroup v2, the namespaces `container.c` already relies on, OverlayFS, `squashfs`, the target's real storage/NIC drivers, rtnetlink/netfilter-free networking — no support for anything this project doesn't use). Built with a real GCC toolchain, not TCC — the same "TCC governs our own code, not unmodified upstream software" precedent Phase 7's routing daemons, Phase 8's `dnsmasq`, Phase 9's `openssl`, and Phase 10's own build-toolchain bootstrap already established. Likely reuses Phase 10's `pkg/bootstrap` GCC-staging approach rather than inventing a second one.
- **Partition layout, 5 GPT partitions on the target disk:**

  | # | Role | Filesystem | Mutability | Contents |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | 1 | ESP | FAT32 | writable (bootloader-managed) | `systemd-boot`, kernel image(s), loader entries |
  | 2 | Root A | squashfs | read-only | kernel-paired control plane: `kanxeod` + its direct runtime deps |
  | 3 | Root B | squashfs | read-only | the other root slot, identical role to (2) |
  | 4 | Config | ext4 | writable | `kanxeod`'s own durable state — `networks.json`, `dns_records.json`, `pki/`, the package registry — plus NIC/hostname config from the installer. Exactly today's `/var/lib/kanxeo/` layout, minus container/image data, split onto its own partition so it survives every OS update/rollback untouched |
  | 5 | Containers | ext4 | writable | the package manager's canonical base image, every container's upper/work/merged dirs — all workload data, likewise untouched by OS updates/rollback |

  Root A/B sizing depends on the actual built image size (Part 1's own output); config and containers get generous-but-modest and "rest of disk" respectively. Not pinned further until Part 1 produces a real image to measure.
- **Networking at boot: static IP, set once during install**, applied with the `rtnetlink` primitives `netplane/` already has — no new dependency, no protocol to hand-roll or shell out for. Consistent with a fixed-hardware deployment where the address is planned in advance, not discovered by walking up with DHCP.
- **Installer partitioning: real, interactive `cfdisk`.** The operator creates and sizes partitions themselves and marks each one's role via cfdisk's own GPT partition-type/name menu; the installer then reads the resulting table back (`sfdisk -d` or `/sys/class/block`) and matches roles by type/name — no separate "which number is root A" prompt, less room for operator error than either a fully-scripted non-interactive layout or a manual number-picking step.
- **Disk selection: multi-disk aware.** The installer enumerates available block devices and the operator picks the target before `cfdisk` runs, rather than assuming a single disk is present.
- **PID 1 detection: explicit, not inferred.** `kanxeod` gains a `--init-mode` flag (set via the kernel command line / loader entry) that gates the boot-init responsibilities (mounting `/proc`/`/sys`, bringing up the NIC, reading the config partition, health-signaling the bootloader) — deliberately not a `getpid() == 1` auto-check, so this entire code path can be exercised by an automated test inside a namespace/container the same way `test_harness.c` already tests namespace logic, without needing to actually be a real machine's PID 1 to test it.
- **Update scope: included now, not deferred.** Writing a new image to the *inactive* slot and flipping the active pointer is nearly the same operation as the installer's own initial write — building it now, rather than punting to a "Phase 12," keeps the A/B mechanism genuinely exercised rather than theoretical.

See ADR-0014 for the full reasoning behind the squashfs-A/B-with-native-boot-counting decision specifically — the one call here that's both significant and hard to reverse once real installs exist on real disks.

**Verified, before any of this touched real hardware:** the build/boot-test tooling this phase needs (`qemu-system-x86`, `ovmf`, `systemd-boot-efi`, `squashfs-tools`, `mtools`, plus kernel-build prerequisites `flex`/`bison`/`libelf-dev`) is installed and confirmed working in the dev environment. Two real constraints found empirically, not guessed at: this LXC container has no `/dev/kvm`, so QEMU boot-testing runs software-emulated (TCG) rather than hardware-accelerated — correct but slow; and it exposes no `/dev/loop*` at all, so `test_boot.c`'s disk assembly works entirely at raw byte offsets into the image file (`sfdisk` operates on a plain file directly; the ESP is built as a standalone FAT32 file via `mtools`' `mcopy`/`mmd`, a real userspace FAT implementation needing no mount; both pieces are then written into the disk image at their exact partition offsets, parsed from `sfdisk -d`) rather than the originally-planned loop-device-plus-`mount()` approach. Both are host-level LXC config changes outside this project's own scope, not bugs in the boot chain itself.

Proposed part breakdown, same "verify each part before starting the next" discipline every multi-part phase before this one used:

- **Part 1 (done):** kernel built from mainline source with a minimal defconfig, a minimal `squashfs` root containing just `kanxeod`, and `systemd-boot` — booting end to end in QEMU (software-emulated, no `/dev/kvm` in this environment) to `kanxeod` running in `--init-mode` and reaching a healthy state. No A/B, no installer, no real hardware yet — just proving the boot chain itself works.

  Kernel: `image/kernel/qemu-part1.config`, a hand-curated `merge_config.sh`-format fragment (not a full generated `.config` — ~40 intentional options instead of a ~60KB mostly-autogenerated file), applied over `make allnoconfig` against kernel 6.18.40 (current longterm at the time). Every option traces to a concrete requirement — cgroup v2 controllers matching `src/cgroup.c`'s actual `memory.max`/`pids.max`/`cpu.max` writes exactly, `EFI_STUB` for `systemd-boot` to chainload directly, `SQUASHFS`+`DEVTMPFS_MOUNT` for the root itself, `VIRTIO_*` for QEMU's virtual hardware (Part 1's own fixed/known "target"). Built with the system's real GCC, not TCC (ADR-0001's "TCC governs our own code, not unmodified upstream software" precedent, same reasoning that already let BIRD/FRR/`dnsmasq`/`openssl`/Phase 10's build-toolchain bootstrap through).

  Root image: new `build/mkbootroot` (`image/src/mkbootroot.c`), reusing `test_image_fixture_build()` (`test/`) for the `kanxeod`+`ld.so`+`libc.so.6` staging rather than a second implementation of the same logic — that function's own contract ("shared by every test that needs a real, working image") already covered this need exactly. Produces an 848KB `squashfs` image: `kanxeod`, its two dynamic-link dependencies, and empty `/proc`, `/sys`, `/dev`, `/var/lib/kanxeo` mountpoints.

  `kanxeod --init-mode` (`daemon/src/main.c`): a new flag in the existing argv-parsing loop, gating a new `boot_init()` that mounts `proc`/`sysfs`/`cgroup2` (the same flags `mountns.c` already uses for each container's own `/proc` — one already-correct flag set, not a second one) and a `tmpfs` at `BASE_DIR` (Part 1's explicit stand-in for the real config partition Part 3 mounts there instead — same mount point, nothing downstream changes when that lands), then brings `lo` up via `rtnl_link_set_up()` (`netplane/` — already existed, reused directly, not a second way to change link state) before falling through into the *completely unmodified* existing startup sequence (`ensure_dir`/`network_init`/`dns_init`/`pki_init`/`pkg_init`/`registry_init`/socket/`epoll`) — the concrete proof this isn't a boot-mode stub: that real sequence has to genuinely succeed on a freshly booted kernel for the test to pass.

  Verified end to end by new `test/test_boot.c`: assembles the throwaway 2-partition disk (ESP + one root slot — the real 5-partition A/B layout is Part 3's job, not this test's), boots it, and scrapes the serial console for the exact same `"kanxeod listening on ..."` line every other test in this suite already treats as daemon-ready — no new "health" concept invented for this part.

  **Two real bugs found via an actual boot, not caught by reasoning about the code beforehand** — exactly this project's standing "verify empirically" discipline (ADR-0008/ADR-0009) applied to a whole new domain: (1) `bind(127.0.0.1)` failed with `EADDRNOTAVAIL` — a fresh kernel boot leaves `lo` administratively down, nothing had ever needed to bring it up before since every prior phase ran as a guest on an already-fully-booted host. `kanxeod` died, and PID 1 dying panics the kernel unconditionally — a correct kernel behavior surfacing what was actually a missing boot-init step, not a bug in the boot chain itself. Fixed by the `rtnl_link_set_up()` call above. (2) `devtmpfs: error mounting -2` — the squashfs root had no `/dev` directory for the kernel's own devtmpfs auto-mount to target. Fixed by adding it to `mkbootroot`'s staged tree alongside `/proc`/`/sys`.

  Also found and fixed before either of those, during initial kernel-config curation (not caught by a "boot it and see" cycle, but by verifying the checked-in config fragment reproduces cleanly from a clean `allnoconfig` — the same "don't trust a config is right until proven" posture applied one level earlier): the first attempt silently dropped `EFI_STUB`/`VIRTIO_BLK`/`VIRTIO_NET`/`SQUASHFS` entirely, because `scripts/config --enable` sets a symbol directly without checking Kconfig's real dependency graph, and a subsequent `make olddefconfig` re-derives everything through that graph and silently turns unsatisfiable options back off. Root causes: `EFI` depends on `ACPI` (not yet enabled); `VIRTIO_BLK`/`VIRTIO_NET` live under `menuconfig BLK_DEV`/`NETDEVICES` gates (not just `VIRTIO`); `SQUASHFS` lives under `if MISC_FILESYSTEMS`. Fixed by enabling the actual gating symbols, then re-verified by reproducing the *checked-in* fragment from scratch against a clean `allnoconfig` — which caught a second miss the same way (`CONFIG_BINFMT_ELF`, enabled interactively but initially left out of the written-down fragment; `allnoconfig` ignores Kconfig `default y` statements, so this one needs to be explicit despite looking like it should be on by default).

  Zero warnings; `test_boot` re-run 3 consecutive times with identical passing results; the full pre-existing 13-binary suite re-run once more after a clean rebuild to confirm the additive, default-off `--init-mode` flag introduced no regression to any existing invocation of `kanxeod`.

- **Part 2 (done):** A/B write + `systemd-boot`'s boot-counter + automatic rollback, still in QEMU — deliberately break the "active" slot's confirmation and confirm the system falls back to the other one with no operator involved.

  Confirmed with the user before writing any code, the same "ask, iterate, reflect" cadence part 1's own architecture questions used: the failure simulated is a *valid* boot where `kanxeod` deliberately never confirms (`--simulate-unhealthy-boot`), not a corrupted/unmountable root — the real failure class this mechanism exists for (a bad write that boots but is broken), not just "doesn't boot at all." Slot identification is an explicit `--slot=a`/`--slot=b` kernel param, the same explicit-over-inferred posture `--init-mode` itself already established. The test harness launches QEMU fresh for each power-on attempt against the same persistent disk image rather than relying on an in-VM reboot loop — controllable and inspectable, matching this project's existing poll-and-check test style.

  `daemon/src/main.c`: new `--slot=`/`--simulate-unhealthy-boot` flags (same `strncmp`/`strcmp` argv-parsing shape as every existing flag). `boot_init()` gains one more mount: the ESP itself (`vfat`, fixed QEMU device `/dev/vda1`, same fixed-layout posture `root=/dev/vda2` already has), needed to reach the loader entry `confirm_boot(slot)` renames. New `confirm_boot()`: `opendir()`s `/boot/loader/entries`, finds the one entry starting with `kanxeo-<slot>`, `rename()`s it to the bare `kanxeo-<slot>.conf` — stripping whatever `+<tries-left>[-<tries-done>]` counter `systemd-boot`'s own Automatic Boot Assessment had written, a plain filesystem rename, not a `bootctl` call, ADR-0014's posture applied for real. Called right after the existing `"kanxeod listening on..."` line and before the reactor loop starts — "about to serve traffic" is the honest definition of healthy this confirms. A `confirm_boot()` failure logs and continues rather than tearing down an already-healthy daemon, the same best-effort/skip-and-log posture `dns_register`/`pki_issue`'s own collision handling already established (Phase 8/9).

  `image/src/mkbootroot.c` gains one more empty mountpoint, `/boot` — the image's actual *contents* are identical regardless of which slot it's written to, since slot behavior is entirely determined by each loader entry's own `init=` argv, not anything baked into the squashfs.

  `test/test_disk_image.h`/`.c` (new): every disk/partition/ESP/QEMU-boot primitive `test_boot.c` (part 1) had already written generically, extracted once `test_boot_ab.c` became a second real consumer of the exact same logic — subprocess runners, `sfdisk_dump_offset()`, `write_at_offset()`, `mtools` wrappers (`esp_mkfs`/`esp_mmd`/`esp_mcopy_in`/`esp_mren`), and a generalized `qemu_boot_capture()` that returns the full captured serial text to the caller instead of deciding pass/fail internally — the same "extract when a genuine second consumer appears" moment `test_image_fixture.c`'s own origin already established for this project. `test_boot.c` refactored onto it with zero intended behavior change, re-verified (3 consecutive passes) specifically to prove the extraction was clean.

  New `test/test_boot_ab.c`: a 3-partition disk (ESP + root A + root B, both slots carrying the *same* squashfs image — only the loader entries differ), slot A's entry `kanxeo-a+3.conf` (3 tries, `--simulate-unhealthy-boot`), slot B's `kanxeo-b.conf` (no counter, already the confirmed-good fallback). Repeated power-on attempts against the persistent disk, each one scraping the serial console for a new `"init-mode: slot=..."` log line (needed since `--simulate-unhealthy-boot` still reaches the normal "listening" line — only the confirm step is skipped, so that line alone can no longer distinguish which slot booted) and listing `/loader/entries` directly against the disk image via `mtools`' `mdir -i disk.img@@<offset>` (no mount, same `@@offset` addressing the ESP write already used) to observe the counter decrementing between attempts.

  **A real, non-obvious mechanics gap found via an actual run, not assumed correct from documentation** — this project's "verify empirically" discipline paying off a third time in this phase: the first attempt, with `loader.conf`'s `default` set to a plain `kanxeo-*` glob, booted straight to slot B on the very first try, not slot A — a glob only says "these are all valid candidates," it expresses no priority between them, and `systemd-boot`'s own tie-break didn't favor the freshly-written slot. Fixed per the Boot Loader Specification's actual mechanism for this exact scenario: both entries get a matching `sort-key` (grouping them as "the same boot item across versions") plus explicit `version` fields, slot A's version set higher than slot B's — an unambiguous, deliberate priority signal instead of leaning on filename sort-order accidents. Re-verified end to end: attempt 1 boots A (`+3`→`+2-1`), attempt 2 boots A again (`+1-2`), attempt 3 boots A again (`+0-3`, exhausted), attempt 4 automatically lands on B — the real rollback, observed, not inferred.

  Two more real kernel-config gaps found via actual boots, same discipline as part 1's own bugs: `/boot: No such device` (the kernel had never been asked to support `vfat`/`FAT` at all, since part 1 never mounted the ESP from inside the guest) fixed with `CONFIG_FAT_FS`/`CONFIG_VFAT_FS`; then `FAT-fs (vda1): codepage cp437 not found` (VFAT needs NLS codepage tables to interpret filenames — nothing had enabled any) fixed with `CONFIG_NLS`/`CONFIG_NLS_CODEPAGE_437`/`CONFIG_NLS_ISO8859_1`, added to the same `image/kernel/qemu-part1.config` fragment part 1 already established (one kernel across the whole phase, not a second config file per part) and re-verified by reproducing it from a clean `allnoconfig` again before trusting it.

  Zero warnings; `test_boot_ab` and `test_boot` each re-run 3 consecutive times with identical passing results after a clean rebuild; the full pre-existing 13-binary suite re-run once more to confirm zero regression from the additive `--slot=`/`--simulate-unhealthy-boot` flags and the shared-module extraction.
- **Part 3 (done):** the installer itself — disk selection, `cfdisk` flow with GPT-type-based role assignment, partition formatting, image writes to all five partitions, static IP configuration.

  Confirmed with the user before writing any code, four rounds: the installer runs inside a QEMU guest against a second attached virtio disk for its own testing, using ordinary `mount()`/`mkfs` — the loop-device workaround `test_disk_image.c` needed (part 1) only exists because that code builds a disk *image file* on this dev LXC for QEMU's own benefit; it doesn't apply inside a guest, where a second disk is a real block device, so the installer's own code is simpler than the test harness's (no `mtools`, no raw byte-offset math). It's a new standalone binary (`kanxeo-install`, `image/src/kanxeo-install.c`), not a `kanxeod` mode, matching `mkbootroot.c`'s existing precedent. Input is CLI flags (`--disk=`, `--ip=`, `--prefix=`, `--gateway=`), not interactive prompts — scriptable, testable by driving the real built binary with real arguments. The very first install is written with a boot-attempt counter too, exactly like any future update (part 2's mechanism, reused uniformly) — root B starts genuinely empty and stays that way until the first real update ever writes there.

  **The installer needs to be bootable itself** — there's no other way for an automated test (or a real operator) to actually run it against a target disk. It's a second `init=` target, the exact same kernel+`systemd-boot` infrastructure parts 1–2 already proved, just pointed at `/bin/kanxeo-install` in its own squashfs image instead of `/bin/kanxeod` — consistent with part 4's own already-written scope note that "the installer environment itself needs to boot from *something*." Part 3 builds that environment's payload; part 4 turns it into a distributable ISO.

  GPT partition **names** (not just types) disambiguate the five roles, since root A/B/config/containers would otherwise all share a generic "Linux filesystem" type: `kanxeo-esp` (type `uefi`, the one hard UEFI requirement), `kanxeo-root-a`, `kanxeo-root-b`, `kanxeo-config`, `kanxeo-containers`. `kanxeo-install` reads these back via `sfdisk -d`, matching each `name="..."` field to find its device — a small parser reimplemented as real installer code (not linked from `test/`, the same production/test boundary `pkg.c`'s own `copy_file_simple()` vs. `test_image_fixture.c`'s `copy_file()` already established in an earlier audit this session).

  `kanxeo-install`'s flow: confirm the target disk exists; unless `--skip-partition`, shell out to the real, interactive `cfdisk` (real software, not hand-rolled — the same posture as every other external tool this project uses) and block until the operator exits it; read the partition table back and match all five roles, failing clearly if any are missing; `mkfs.vfat` the ESP, `mkfs.ext4` the config and containers partitions (root A gets no `mkfs` — the squashfs image *is* the filesystem, written raw; root B gets nothing at all, genuinely empty); mount the ESP (a real block device now, so a plain `mount()` — no `mtools` needed) and copy in `systemd-bootx64.efi`, the kernel, and a loader entry for `kanxeo-root-a` carrying an initial tries-counter; write the bundled root-A squashfs raw onto its partition; mount the config partition and write a small `ip=`/`prefix=`/`gateway=` `net.conf`; mount the containers partition just long enough to confirm the format succeeded (no content — `kanxeod`'s own existing `ensure_dir()`/`pkg_init()` machinery populates it at first real boot, the same "fall through to unmodified existing logic" precedent parts 1–2 already established); print `"kanxeo-install: install complete"` and exit (PID 1 exiting still panics the kernel — expected and harmless, the operator reboots into the freshly-installed system regardless).

  A real subtlety threaded through carefully, not glossed over: the loader entry's `root=` value (and every future boot's own partition access) is about the *installed* system's future boot, once the installer's own disk is gone and the target is the only disk present — not derived from whatever device path the installer itself saw the target disk at during install (`/dev/vdb`, the second disk, alongside the installer's own `/dev/vda`). Both use a fixed convention (`/dev/vda` + partition number, matching parts 1–2's own already-fixed `root=/dev/vda2`), since this phase's whole scope is fixed/known target hardware, not arbitrary disk enumeration order.

  `daemon/src/main.c` gained the other half of "static IP configured at install time": `boot_init()` now also (non-fatally — parts 1–2's own throwaway disks have no partition 4 at all, and that must stay a normal, inert boot) mounts the config partition and, if a `net.conf` is present, applies it — new `find_nic()` (scans `/sys/class/net` for the first real, non-loopback interface rather than hardcoding a name, since a fixed name could differ on part 4's real hardware even though QEMU's virtio-net was confirmed empirically to get `eth0`), `parse_net_conf()`, and `apply_static_ip()`, which reuses the exact `rtnl_open()`/`rtnl_link_set_up()`/`rtnl_addr_add_ipv4()`/`rtnl_route_add_default_ipv4()` primitives (`netplane/`) `boot_init()`'s own `lo` bring-up already established — no new networking code, no ioctl-based second path.

  New shared test primitives (`test/test_disk_image.h`/`.c`): `qemu_boot_capture()` gained an optional second disk and an optional virtio-net device (both off by default for parts 1–2's existing callers); `extract_partition()` (the reverse of `write_at_offset()` — pulls a partition's raw bytes into a standalone file, needed for `debugfs`, which unlike `mtools` has no `@@offset` addressing and needs a filesystem image starting at byte 0); `build_squashfs()` (test-side counterpart to `mkbootroot`'s own private squashfs builder, needed for `test_installer.c`'s second, different bootable image). A real, if minor, hygiene gap fixed while in this area: every boot test's `mkdtemp()` workdir (a disk image plus a squashfs build, tens to hundreds of MB) was never cleaned up — 16 leftover directories, ~1GB, found accumulated from this phase's own development so far. Fixed with a shared `rm_tree()` (the same `nftw()`-based pattern `test_overlay.c` already established), called on every test's successful exit only — a failure's artifacts stay, deliberately, since they're worth keeping to debug.

  New `test/test_installer.c`, two QEMU sessions: **(1)** the installer's own bootable image (`kanxeo-install` + its full real-tool closure — `cfdisk`/`sfdisk`/`mkfs.vfat`/`mkfs.ext4` and every library each actually links against, checked directly against this host and staged the same way `dnsmasq`'s own closure was in Phase 8 — plus the bundled payload: the control-plane squashfs, kernel, `systemd-bootx64.efi`) boots alongside a blank target disk pre-partitioned by the test via `sfdisk`, standing in for what a real `cfdisk` session would have left (`cfdisk`'s own curses UI can't be scripted, and it's real, unmodified software this project doesn't need to re-verify — `--skip-partition` exists for exactly this, and for real unattended/scripted provisioning). **(2)** After verifying from the host side what the installer actually wrote — the ESP's loader entry via `mtools`, root A's raw bytes compared byte-for-byte against the original squashfs, the config partition's `net.conf` via `debugfs` (no mount anywhere in this test, the same posture parts 1–2 already established) — the target disk boots *alone*, for real, with a virtio-net device attached this time, and the test asserts the static IP was genuinely applied, not just that the config file was written. "Install" and "boot what was installed" are the same real code paths already proven in parts 1–2, not a separate, untested assumption.

  **Two real bugs found via actual runs, not caught by reasoning about the code beforehand:** (1) `kanxeo-install` itself needs the same minimal PID-1 boot shape `kanxeod`'s own `boot_init()` already has — the first attempt failed with `/proc: No such file or directory` because neither `early_mounts()` (mounting `/proc`/`/sys` before anything else runs) nor the `/proc`/`/sys`/`/dev` mountpoint directories in the installer's own staged image existed at all; both were missing from the very first design pass. Fixed by adding `early_mounts()` to `kanxeo-install.c` (mirroring `boot_init()`) and the three mountpoints to `test_installer.c`'s own image-staging step. (2) The test's first pass at verifying the static-IP path only asserted the config file was written, not that it was actually *applied* — the installed system's second boot session never attached a virtio-net device, so `find_nic()` correctly (and harmlessly, logged, non-fatal) found nothing. Caught by re-reading what the test actually proved, not by a failure: strengthened by attaching a NIC to that session and asserting `"init-mode: applied static ip ..."` — plus the exact configured address and gateway — actually appears.

  Zero warnings; `test_installer` re-run 3 consecutive times with identical passing results; `test_boot` and `test_boot_ab` (parts 1–2) each re-run 3 consecutive times to confirm zero regression from the kernel-config additions (`EXT4_FS`) and `boot_init()`'s new network-config-reading step; the full pre-existing 13-binary suite re-run once more after a clean rebuild.
- **Part 4 (done):** ISO packaging — the installer environment finally becomes a real, distributable `.iso`, the concrete artifact an operator hands to their own hypervisor. Confirmed with the user before writing any code: since this dev LXC has no physical hardware, "bare-metal validation" isn't literally possible from here — this part's own completion bar is booting the *real* `.iso` artifact via QEMU's CD-ROM emulation (`-cdrom`, a genuinely different, more realistic path than the raw-disk-image testing parts 1–3 used); the user's own VM install is the real-world follow-up, outside this session's own reach. The installer stays shell-less, consistent with this phase's already-established fixed/known-hardware scope.

  **A working technical approach was found via direct empirical spiking, not guessed from documentation** — this project's "verify before committing to a design" discipline paying off a fourth time in this phase. Hand-tuning `xorriso`'s raw El Torito/GPT-hybrid flags directly (`-append_partition ... 0xef`, `-eltorito-alt-boot`) proved genuinely fiddly and kept failing (`BdsDxe: failed to load Boot0001 ... Not Found`) even once the referenced boot image was a real FAT filesystem rather than a bare `.efi` blob — OVMF found the device but never got a usable boot catalog out of it. **`grub-mkrescue`** (the standard tool every major distro uses for exactly this, the same "real software, not hand-rolled" posture as every other external tool this project shells out to) produced a working UEFI-bootable hybrid ISO on the first real attempt. The kernel mounts the ISO9660 media **directly as root** (`root=/dev/sr0 rootfstype=iso9660 ro`) — no initramfs, no squashfs, the same posture every other root filesystem in this phase already has; verified end to end (`VFS: Mounted root (iso9660 filesystem) readonly on device 11:0.`). GRUB is only ever the *installer media's* own bootloader, a one-time install-time-only role — `systemd-boot`'s role on the *installed* target disk (ADR-0014's counted A/B mechanism) is completely unaffected; `kanxeo-install` still writes `systemd-boot`'s own ESP structure onto the target disk exactly as proven in part 3.

  Four new kernel config options, found and verified the same way: `CONFIG_ATA`+`CONFIG_SATA_AHCI` (QEMU's `-cdrom` attaches through Q35's AHCI SATA controller as an ATAPI device), `CONFIG_BLK_DEV_SR` (the SCSI CD-ROM block driver — ATAPI devices are handled through the SCSI subsystem in Linux), `CONFIG_ISO9660_FS`, added to the same `image/kernel/qemu-part1.config` fragment every prior part already used (one kernel across the whole phase).

  **A real, non-obvious production UX gap surfaced while designing this, not discovered later**: `kanxeo-install` takes CLI flags, not interactive prompts (part 3's own confirmed decision) — but a real operator booting from removable media can't know the target disk path or desired IP in advance, and there's no shell to edit anything at runtime. The answer needs no new interactivity inside `kanxeo-install` itself (consistent with staying shell-less): GRUB's own well-known boot-menu command-line editing (press `e`, adjust the args, `Ctrl-X` to boot). The shipped `grub.cfg` carries a deliberately-invalid placeholder disk path (`/dev/CHANGEME`) that `kanxeo-install`'s existing `stat()` check already fails safely on if the operator forgets to edit it, rather than silently doing the wrong thing.

  New production tool `image/src/mkinstalleriso.c` (`build/mkinstalleriso <staging-dir> <kanxeo-install-bin> <bzImage> <control-plane-squashfs> <out.iso> <kernel-args>`) — the last argument is everything after `init=/bin/kanxeo-install --`, so the *same* tool produces both the real, shippable ISO (called with the `/dev/CHANGEME` placeholder args) and `test_installer.c`'s own verification ISO (real test values + `--skip-partition`), one implementation, not two. Stages the same content `test_installer.c`'s own `build_installer_image()` used to build directly (removed — see below): `kanxeo-install` + `ld.so`/`libc.so.6`, the real tools it shells out to (`cfdisk`/`sfdisk`/`mkfs.vfat`/`mkfs.ext4`) plus their full `ldd` closures, the target-disk payload (`systemd-bootx64.efi`, the control-plane squashfs), the empty mountpoints — plus, new this part, `/boot/kanxeo-bzImage` and a generated `/boot/grub/grub.cfg`. `kanxeo-install.c`'s own `BZIMAGE_SRC` moved from `/payload/` to `/boot/` — one copy of the kernel serves both GRUB's own boot and the file copied onto the target disk's ESP, not two. Mirrors `mkbootroot.c`'s own precedent of linking `test/test_image_fixture.c` for staging (both are dev-machine-only build tools, never shipped/run on a target — unlike `kanxeo-install.c` itself, which deliberately doesn't).

  `test/test_disk_image.c`'s `qemu_boot_capture()` had grown past the point where more positional parameters stayed readable (this part needs a `-cdrom`-vs-`-drive` choice for the first disk, on top of the existing second-disk and NIC options already added in part 3) — refactored to a `struct qemu_boot_opts`. All four existing call sites updated; re-verified with zero behavior change.

  `test/test_installer.c` simplified, not just extended: its own private `build_installer_image()`/`build_boot_esp()` are gone entirely — session 1 now calls the real `build/mkinstalleriso` and boots the actual resulting `.iso` via `-cdrom`, a strictly more faithful test than part 3 shipped (which built a private, parallel disk-image approximation of the installer environment rather than the real artifact).

  **A self-inflicted environment bug found and fixed along the way, not a code defect**: an early exploratory `xorriso`/QEMU spike accidentally pointed a `-drive if=pflash,...,file=` argument directly at the *shared system template* `/usr/share/OVMF/OVMF_VARS_4M.fd` instead of a private copy, permanently writing a phantom "boot from DVD-ROM" NVRAM entry into it — silently breaking every other test in this project that copies from that same template (all of them). Diagnosed by comparing a full boot log against a previously-known-good one and noticing OVMF was trying to load a nonexistent `Boot0001 "UEFI QEMU DVD-ROM"` entry before ever reaching the real disk; fixed with `apt-get install --reinstall ovmf` to restore the pristine package file, re-verified against `test_boot`/`test_boot_ab` immediately afterward. A reminder embedded in this project's own practice, not just its code: every throwaway exploratory command still touches shared state if it's not careful, even outside the repository itself.

  Verified end to end in new `test/test_installer.c` scenarios: session 1 boots the real `.iso` via `-cdrom` against a blank, pre-partitioned target disk (`--skip-partition`, standing in for a real `cfdisk` session); host-side verification confirms the ESP's loader entry, root A's raw bytes byte-for-byte against the original squashfs, and the config partition's `net.conf` via `debugfs` (no mount anywhere in this test, consistent with every other part in this phase); session 2 boots the target disk *alone*, with a NIC attached this time, and confirms the installed system reaches `"kanxeod listening on..."` with the static IP genuinely applied — the literal, complete install-to-running-system pipeline, not an assumption stitched together from separately-tested pieces.

  Zero warnings; `test_installer` re-run 3 consecutive times with identical passing results; `test_boot`/`test_boot_ab` (parts 1–2) each re-run 3 consecutive times to confirm zero regression from the `qemu_boot_capture()` struct refactor and the kernel-config additions; the full pre-existing 13-binary suite re-run once more after a clean rebuild; the real, shippable `build/kanxeo-install.iso` built manually with production placeholder args and confirmed to exist as a real artifact.
- **Part 5 (done):** Secure Boot for the installed system — a real, user-reported defect in the `v1.1.0` ISO (`BdsDxe: ... Access Denied` on the user's own VM, Secure Boot enabled) turned into this part's own scope once root-caused: `grub-mkrescue`'s self-built `BOOTX64.EFI` is unsigned, and even a signed `shim`+`grub` chain (spiked first) still fails one layer deeper — Secure Boot also requires a *signed kernel*, and this project has no CA relationship to get one signed conventionally. See ADR-0015 for the full shim+MOK design and the bootstrap chicken/egg reasoning that shapes it: the installer media's own boot stays unsigned (Secure Boot must be off for that one, disposable boot — unavoidable, not a regression), while the *installed* system becomes genuinely Secure-Boot-capable after one operator-confirmed MOK enrollment, with zero further exceptions afterward.

  A project-owned signing key (`image/keys/kanxeo-signing.{key,crt,cer}`, generated once, persisted deliberately) signs `systemd-boot` and the kernel via `sbsign` at ISO-build time (`image/src/mkinstalleriso.c`, two new required arguments). Debian's pre-signed `shim` and `MokManager` (`/usr/lib/shim/{shimx64,mmx64}.efi.signed`) are staged unmodified. `image/src/kanxeo-install.c`'s `populate_esp()` now writes the full chain onto the *target* ESP — `EFI/BOOT/BOOTX64.EFI` (shim), `EFI/BOOT/grubx64.efi` (Kanxeo-signed systemd-boot — deliberately kept under that name; confirmed via `strings` on the real shim binary that `\\grubx64.efi` is its hardcoded second-stage lookup, regardless of what's actually inside the file), `EFI/BOOT/mmx64.efi` (MokManager) — and a new `enroll_signing_key()` mounts `efivarfs` and runs `mokutil --import`, prompting once for a password reused at the next boot to confirm enrollment.

  **Two real, non-obvious facts found via direct empirical testing, not assumed:** (1) Secure Boot enforcement in OVMF is gated by the **vars file's own PK-enrollment state**, not the CODE build variant — `OVMF_CODE_4M.secboot.fd` + a fresh, not-yet-enrolled vars file does *not* enforce, while plain `OVMF_CODE_4M.fd` + a `.ms.fd`-templated (already-enrolled) vars file *does* — confirmed by isolating each variable independently. This directly shaped `qemu_boot_capture()`'s new `secure_boot` option and, more importantly, the test design: session 1 (the installer boot) needed a way to stay unenforced while *still* using the exact same, already-User-Mode vars file sessions 2–3 read (so the pending MOK request lands where shim will actually look for it) — solved with QEMU's own `-kernel` direct injection (`direct_kernel` on `qemu_boot_opts`), confirmed to bypass firmware's normal `LoadImage`-based Secure Boot check entirely regardless of vars state. This is a pure test-harness convenience standing in for "Secure Boot off for this one boot" on real hardware — `kanxeo-install`'s own code runs identically either way. (2) `mokutil`'s own `ldd` closure was missing `libdl.so.2` in the first pass (`mokutil: error while loading shared libraries: libdl.so.2`) — caught by an actual run, not code review; fixed in `mkinstalleriso.c`'s `g_lib_closure[]`.

  shim's real MokManager UI (discovered by driving a live run interactively — piped scripted input, observing the raw captured screen output — the same technique already used earlier this session to diagnose the original bug) was scripted into `test_installer.c`'s new session 2: "Press any key to perform MOK management" (a 10s countdown otherwise falls through to a *safe* failure, confirmed separately: "Verification failed: (0x1A) Security Violation", not a worse outcome) → "Perform MOK management" (Continue boot / **Enroll MOK** / Enroll key from disk / Enroll hash from disk) → "View key 0 / **Continue**" → "Enroll the key(s)? No / **Yes**" → "Password:" (the same password `enroll_signing_key()` used) → back at "Perform MOK management", now offering **Reboot**. `qemu_boot_capture()` gained `struct qemu_scripted_input` for this — event-driven (matches on captured output, not sleep-timed), a real, reusable capability for scripting any future interactive pre-boot UI, not just this one.

  Three QEMU sessions in `test_installer.c` now: install (direct-kernel-booted, stages the MOK request), MOK-confirm (`secure_boot=1`, scripted through shim/MokManager), and the real final boot (`secure_boot=1`, confirms `kanxeod` comes up with the static IP applied) — proving the full, real mechanism end to end, not just its individual pieces.

  `image/kernel/qemu-part1.config` gained `CONFIG_EFIVAR_FS` (for `/sys/firmware/efi/efivars`, which `mokutil` needs).

  Zero warnings; `test_installer` re-run 3 consecutive times with identical passing results; `test_boot`/`test_boot_ab` each re-run 3 consecutive times to confirm zero regression from the `qemu_boot_opts` additions; the full pre-existing binary suite re-run once more after a clean rebuild; the real, shippable `build/kanxeo-install.iso` rebuilt with the new signing-key arguments.

- **Part 6 (done): real-world install fixes.** The user's own real Proxmox install of the `v1.2.0` ISO surfaced a run of genuine, previously-untested defects — each one lived in a path the automated suite had always bypassed for a real reason at the time (an interactive UI that seemingly couldn't be scripted, or a QEMU-only test shortcut), and each one got closed with real test coverage, not just a patch:
  - `grub.cfg`'s `timeout=0` booted the boot menu instantly, with no window to press `e` and edit `--disk=`/`--ip=`/... — bumped to `10s`. Both the installer media and the installed system's own boot were also only ever printing to `ttyS0` (serial), leaving an operator on Proxmox's default *display* console looking at what appeared to be a hang — both now print to `tty0` as well.
  - `cfdisk` (real, interactive partitioning) failed outright with `Error opening terminal: linux.` — its `ncurses` terminal database was never staged, a path `test_installer.c` had always skipped via `--skip-partition` specifically because `cfdisk`'s full-screen UI couldn't be scripted. Replaced with `fdisk`: no terminal-database dependency (confirmed directly: works with `TERMINFO`/`TERMINFO_DIRS` pointed at nonexistent paths), and — unlike `cfdisk` — its line-based UI *can* be scripted, closing the coverage gap instead of just working around it (the exact command sequence was verified against a real `fdisk` first, then matched byte-for-byte via `sfdisk -d` against the project's existing layout).
  - A new `--auto-partition` flag (`kanxeo-install`'s own scripted `sfdisk`, added once retyping the same fixed `fdisk` sequence by hand for every VM/scripted install proved to be pure friction, not a meaningful safety check) makes a from-scratch install fully non-interactive; `test_installer.c`'s main install session now exercises this directly, and its own former host-side pre-partitioning (a second copy of the same layout script) is gone.
  - `kanxeod` has a working `--bind=ADDR` flag, but the generated loader entry never passed it, so a fresh install always listened on `127.0.0.1` only, unreachable from the network, despite `apply_static_ip()` having just configured the real address on `eth0` — `kanxeo-install` now bakes `--bind=<its own --ip=>` into the loader entry (confirmed directly via a spike: `kanxeod listening on 192.168.77.77:7620`).
  - The web dashboard 404'd on every request on the installed system, despite the REST API working fine — `kanxeod`'s `DEFAULT_WEB_ROOT` is a relative path, resolved against PID 1's own CWD (the squashfs root, since PID 1 never `chdir()`s), but `mkbootroot` never staged `web/` into that image at all. Fixed, confirmed directly via `unsquashfs -l` that the files land exactly where `kanxeod` resolves them.
  - A cosmetic-only bug: `mke2fs`'s ext4 label for the containers partition silently overflowed its 16-character limit (`kanxeo-containers` → truncated to `kanxeo-container`) — `kanxeo-install` always identifies partitions by GPT *name*, never this label, so it was never a functional bug; shortened deliberately instead of relying on the silent truncation.

  Zero warnings; `test_installer`/`test_boot`/`test_boot_ab` re-verified (3 consecutive `test_installer` passes) after every fix; the real, shippable `build/kanxeo-install.iso` rebuilt each time.

- **Part 7 (done): graceful shutdown/reboot.** Asked directly, once a real install was up and reachable: "how do I start/stop the OS when it's running live?" `kanxeod` as PID 1 had no clean answer — `SIGTERM`/`SIGINT` broke its event loop and returned `0` from `main()`, which as real PID 1 is exactly "init exited," and the kernel panics on that unconditionally (the same failure mode Part 3 already accepted as harmless for the one-time `kanxeo-install` run, but genuinely not acceptable for a live system). See ADR-0016.

  `kanxeod` now calls the real `reboot(2)` (`RB_POWER_OFF`/`RB_AUTOBOOT`) once the event loop stops, strictly gated behind `--init-mode` — a dev/test `kanxeod` (every `test/*.c` invocation) never reaches it, unchanged. New `POST /v1/system/shutdown` and `/v1/system/reboot` (API-first per ADR-0005); `kanxeoctl shutdown`/`reboot` are pure clients of these. `SIGTERM`/`SIGINT` now default to a graceful poweroff instead of silently panicking under `--init-mode`.

  The core mechanism was verified directly and empirically, not assumed: a minimal standalone init, booted on this exact kernel build via a throwaway ISO9660 image (bypassing `kanxeod`'s own complexity entirely), confirmed both `reboot(RB_POWER_OFF)` ("`reboot: Power down`") and `reboot(RB_AUTOBOOT)` ("`reboot: Restarting system`") complete cleanly, neither panicking. The *full* REST-triggered path could not be exercised end to end by this project's own QEMU test harness — `-netdev user` (SLIRP) doesn't route host-to-guest traffic to a guest's own self-configured static IP, only to its own DHCP-assigned one, which doesn't match how `kanxeod --bind=<ip>` addresses itself. A test-harness limitation specific to this dev LXC's QEMU setup, not a real-world one (bridged networking, e.g. the user's own Proxmox network, has no equivalent restriction) — see ADR-0016's own Consequences for the honest boundary, and revisit if a future session sets up real bridged test networking.

**Phase 11 is now complete — all 7 parts done.** Kanxeo can be built, from mainline kernel source, into a real installer ISO that partitions a disk (interactively or automatically), writes a counted, A/B-rollback-protected control plane, configures networking, boots into a genuinely running system reachable over the network with a working dashboard, does all of that with Secure Boot genuinely enforced end to end after one operator-confirmed key enrollment, and can be cleanly shut down or restarted from the API/CLI/dashboard — proven at every layer in QEMU, and now also confirmed against the user's own real Proxmox VM, the actual real-world checkpoint this phase was always building toward.

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** anything beyond a fixed/known hardware target (broader driver support, dynamic module loading — see `docs/mission/MISSION.md`'s expanded charter for where this is headed: hardware becomes a managed, assignable resource, not a fixed target); disk encryption; a network-based installer (PXE); any update-delivery mechanism beyond "write a new image to the inactive slot locally" (no remote update server, no staged/canary rollout, and no equivalent rolling mechanism for *packages* yet, despite the charter now calling for one); a recovery/troubleshooting shell in the installer environment (confirmed out of scope, consistent with the fixed/known-hardware boundary); input validation on `kanxeo-install`'s own `--ip=`/`--gateway=` values at install time (malformed values only surface at the installed system's first real boot, not a regression — a pre-existing, minor gap); Secure Boot for the *installer media itself* (stays permanently unsigned — a genuine, documented boundary, not an oversight, per ADR-0015's bootstrap reasoning); signing-key rotation/revocation (one key, no expiry handling yet); `kanxeod` reacting to a hypervisor's own ACPI shutdown signal (no `acpid`-equivalent event handling yet — `kanxeoctl shutdown`/`reboot` or the dashboard are the operator-facing answer today, per ADR-0016); disk quotas; the web dashboard's own eventual containerization; multi-host coordination.

## Phase 12 and beyond — the expanded charter

`docs/mission/MISSION.md` was given a genuinely new charter once Phase 11 proved the platform on real hardware: Kanxeo is now explicitly a hardware and workload orchestration platform, not just a container host, with a "desktop"-style web control surface, real device passthrough, non-disruptive rolling delivery for packages as well as the OS, and an eventual multi-host trajectory. Phases are numbered and written up here, in this same format, exactly as each one is actually designed and built — not before.

### Phase 12 part 1 (done): PCI/USB (character/block) device passthrough

The agreed first step of the expanded charter's hardware-passthrough goal: pass a real USB device or a driver-backed PCI device (e.g. an NVMe namespace) directly to a container. Namespace containers (not VMs) need no IOMMU/VFIO handoff — the mechanism `docs/mission/MISSION.md` already committed to is the specific `/dev` node inside the container's own mount namespace, with the cgroup device controller granting access to it.

Two real design forks were confirmed directly with the user before building anything, matching this project's own precedent (ADR-0011) for resolving ambiguous charter wording in writing first:

- **Enforcement:** cgroup v2 has no file-based device controller — `BPF_CGROUP_DEVICE` (an eBPF program attached per-container to the cgroup leaf `cgroup_create()` already opens) is the only kernel mechanism. This is the first eBPF code anywhere in Kanxeo; ADR-0017 documents why it doesn't reopen the networking plane's own "no eBPF" rule (that rule's wording, checked against every occurrence in the repo, is scoped to the packet-forwarding data plane specifically). Hand-assembled in `src/container_dev.c` with no libbpf/clang dependency, matching `netplane/`'s existing hand-rolled-netlink posture. Namespace-only isolation was considered and rejected: this project's containers run as full root with no user namespace and no capability dropping, so `mknod()` of an ungranted device's major:minor would otherwise just work.
- **Discovery:** `daemon/src/device.c` walks `/sys/bus/usb/devices` and `/sys/bus/pci/devices` fresh on every `GET /v1/devices` call (never persisted — real host hardware, not daemon-owned state, the same distinction that already separates networks' ADR-0012 persistence from containers' own restart-non-survival). USB nodes resolve via each device's own `dev` sysfs attribute (confirmed empirically not derivable from `busnum`/`devnum`, which are reassigned on every replug anyway). PCI nodes are driver-specific and not uniform — found via a bounded-depth walk of each PCI device's own sysfs subtree (an NVMe controller owns both its controller node and a separate node per namespace, nested two levels deep; a bridge/root port's own subtree contains a real, further-walkable subdirectory for each downstream device, requiring an explicit "don't re-descend into an independently-enumerable PCI address" guard to avoid duplicate entries — found and fixed via this session's own real hardware, not synthetic test data).

`container_spec` gained `devices[]`/`device_count` (mirroring the existing `nets[]`/`route_spec` pattern exactly); `container_create()` attaches the BPF program before `ns_clone3()` (so the policy covers the child's very first instruction, including its own `mknod()` calls) and `mknod()`s each granted node right after `mountns_pivot()` (the first point the container's `/dev` is genuinely private). `POST /v1/containers` gained a `devices` array of bare ids (the same request-is-references / response-is-full-objects asymmetry `networks` already established); `GET /v1/devices` is read-only (devices aren't daemon-created, unlike networks — no `POST`/`DELETE`). `kanxeoctl device ls` and a repeatable `run --device=ID` flag are pure REST clients, per the API-First Mandate.

Explicitly out of scope for this part: GPU passthrough specifically (same mechanism, a later step); wireless/wired PCI network cards (these present as `netdev`s, not `/dev` nodes — "assigning" one means moving the interface into a container's netns via the *existing* rtnetlink-based network-attachment feature, not this one); disk quotas; live attach/detach on an already-running container (creation-time-only, matching how `networks`/`routes` already work); per-device-access-type granularity (a grant is full read/write/mknod, all-or-nothing); exclusivity (two containers can be granted the same device simultaneously — real hardware sharing at the kernel level, no lock).

Verified with real, not mocked, syscalls: `test/test_devices.c` proves the actual security property through a real `container_create()` — a container with a device granted opens it and `fstat()` confirms the correct major:minor; a second container with a *different* grant is denied `EPERM` on a device it wasn't granted, even though that device is visibly present (baked into the shared base image); a third container requesting no devices at all is byte-for-byte unaffected (No Regressions) — 3 consecutive passes. `test/test_daemon_devices.c` proves the REST/registry/CLI wiring over real HTTP against a running daemon, adapting to whatever real hardware the test host actually has rather than hardcoding a specific device. `image/kernel/qemu-part1.config` gained `CONFIG_BPF_SYSCALL`/`CONFIG_CGROUP_BPF` (confirmed absent before this change) for the shipped kernel; this dev environment's own host kernel already supported `bpf(2)`, so verification needed no kernel rebuild.

A real environment constraint surfaced and documented rather than papered over (ADR-0017's own Consequences): `BPF_CGROUP_DEVICE` checks are hierarchical, and this project's own dev/build environment (a privileged, nested LXC) runs under an ancestor cgroup that only permits a standard device set — `test_devices.c` grants real device numbers (`/dev/null`/`/dev/zero`/`/dev/full`'s own major:minor) rather than synthetic ones for exactly this reason. Real deployment targets have no such ancestor.

### Phase 12 part 2 (done): a real reboot no longer wipes every installed package, network, and image

Found while scoping the next step (installing real software — `bash`, `iproute2`, `bird` — via `pkg install`, which only matters if it survives a reboot): `boot_init()` has mounted a fresh `tmpfs` at `BASE_DIR` (`/var/lib/kanxeo`) on *every* real boot since Phase 11 part 1, and every piece of state this daemon persists lives there — `images/` (including the shared `base` image every `pkg install` build lands its files into), `networks.json`, `dns_records.json`, `pki_certs.json`, `pkg_installed.json`. A full power cycle on a real installed system silently discarded all of it. This was never the intent — the fifth partition `kanxeo-install` already creates and formats for exactly this purpose (`kanxeo-containers`) has been sitting on every installed system's disk, correctly formatted, completely unused, since Phase 11 part 3. See ADR-0018 for the full account, including a stale code comment that had promised this swap since Part 1 without it ever actually happening.

`boot_init()` now mounts the real `kanxeo-containers` partition (`/dev/vda5`, the same fixed-layout convention `ESP_DEVICE`/`CONFIG_DEVICE` already use) at `BASE_DIR`, falling back to `tmpfs` only when that device doesn't exist (Part 1/2's own throwaway test disks). `main()` also gained one `sync()` call, right after state-init and before the listening socket opens, gated on `--init-mode`: QEMU's default write-back disk cache (and, on real hardware, the same class of guest-side dirty-page caching) only guarantees a write reaches the actual disk once the guest flushes, and "listening on" is exactly the line both this project's own tests and a real operator treat as "safe to power-cycle."

Verified end to end, not just "the mount succeeded": `test/test_installer.c` gained a real cross-boot proof — after the installed system's first full boot, the real on-disk `kanxeo-containers` partition is extracted and confirmed to genuinely contain `kanxeod`'s own directories, a marker file is written directly into it (`debugfs -w`, no mount needed, this project's established no-loop-devices posture), and a *completely independent second boot* of the same disk confirms it's still there, byte-for-byte — 3 consecutive passes. Getting there surfaced two more real, non-obvious bugs, both fixed and documented rather than routed around: an earlier draft placed the new `sync()` *after* the "listening on" print, racing the test harness's own kill-on-marker behavior and defeating the fix entirely; and a partition extracted right after a QEMU session still carries a pending ext4 journal from that abrupt stop, which a raw `debugfs` write can have silently reverted by the next real mount's own journal replay unless `e2fsck -fy` forces that replay first.

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** a config-partition-style restore path for anything beyond what the `kanxeo-containers` partition itself now covers; real target hardware's own partition-name-based device lookup (same open item `ESP_DEVICE`/`CONFIG_DEVICE` already carry, per Part 4's own scope boundary).

### Phase 12 part 3 (done): real pkg recipes for bash, iproute2, and bird

With persistence genuinely real (part 2), the first real `.recipe` files: `pkg/recipes/{bash,iproute2,bird}.recipe`, each with a real upstream source URL and a sha256 verified against an independent authority beyond the daemon's own download (a second GNU mirror for bash; kernel.org's own published `sha256sums.asc` for iproute2; Debian's own `.dsc` `Checksums-Sha256` for bird's Debian-repackaged `.orig.tar.xz`, since `bird.network.cz`'s own download page 403s any non-browser `User-Agent`).

Verified by actually building and installing all three through a live daemon — not written from a template and assumed correct. Doing that surfaced a chain of real, generic gaps in the pkgbuild toolchain image (`pkg_bootstrap_build_image()`, `daemon/src/pkg.c`) that would have blocked *any* package's build, not just these three: no `/dev` at all (a build's own `./configure` routinely redirects probes to `/dev/null`); no `/tmp` (autoconf's `config.guess` needs one); several Debian-specific paths a real build reaches for that the existing `/usr/{include,lib,lib64,bin,libexec}` copy never covered — an `/etc/alternatives/awk` symlink target, bison's and autoconf's own runtime M4/macro libraries, and the core Perl modules `autoconf` itself is written in. Each fixed narrowly (staged from the host the same `cp -a`, idempotent way the existing toolchain copy already is — `/etc/alternatives`, `/usr/share/bison`, `/usr/share/autoconf`, `/usr/share/perl`), found and fixed one real build failure at a time rather than guessed at up front. BIRD's own tarball (Debian's repackaged upstream release) needed one more real fix specific to it: no pre-generated `./configure`, requiring `autoreconf -fi` in its own recipe's `pkg_build()`.

Going one step further than "the package installed" — actually running the freshly-built `bash` inside a real container — surfaced Phase 12 part 4's own finding (see below): nothing yet stages a C runtime into the shared `base` image, so nothing installed this way could actually execve() successfully until that part landed too.

### Phase 12 part 4 (done): a C runtime for the shared "base" image, seeded at install time

`pkg_build_completed()` only ever merges a package's own build output into `base/rootfs` — confirmed directly (part 3's own final check) that a freshly pkg-installed `bash` fails outright inside a real container: `child: execve: No such file or directory`, because `/lib64/ld-linux-x86-64.so.2` doesn't exist anywhere in the image. Not specific to bash — this blocks any dynamically-linked package from ever running.

Confirmed with the user directly which of three real options to build (a runtime REST bootstrap action mirroring `pkg bootstrap`; baking it into the boot-image-build tooling; or leaving it as a documented gap): baking it in. Investigating the actual code changed the concrete shape of that from the literal first guess (`mkbootroot.c`, which builds the OS's own control-plane squashfs — a wholly separate artifact from the containers partition where `images/base/rootfs` actually lives) to `mkinstalleriso.c`, which already had exactly the right, proven pattern (`g_lib_closure[]`, a hardcoded host-library-path array staged via `test_image_fixture_add_lib()`, already used for the installer's *own* execution environment) — extended with three more entries (`ld-linux-x86-64.so.2`, `libc.so.6`, `libtinfo.so.6`) into a new `/payload/kanxeo-runtime/` subtree. `kanxeo-install.c`'s own containers-partition block (previously format-check only, see part 2) now copies those three files into `images/base/rootfs/{lib64,lib/x86_64-linux-gnu}` at real install time. See ADR-0019.

Verified two ways: `test/test_installer.c` extracts the real containers partition right after the installer's own first boot — before any `pkg install`, before the independent second-boot persistence check — and confirms all three files are present with correct real byte sizes; separately, a throwaway `base` image manually seeded with the same three files let a real `bash -c` genuinely execve() and run inside a real container, printing its own version string. 3 consecutive `test_installer` passes; `test_boot`/`test_boot_ab` re-verified with no regression.

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** this seeds a fixed, minimal runtime (exactly what bash needs) — not a general dependency-resolution mechanism; a future package needing a library beyond this set is a real, known gap (extending the same set the same way is the straightforward path when that's actually needed, not attempted speculatively now).

### Phase 12 part 5 (done): `pkg install` targets an explicit image, not always "base"

With `bash`/`iproute2`/`bird` genuinely installable and runnable (parts 3-4), the user's actual use case surfaced the next real gap directly: building a `router`-flavored image carrying them without every other container on the shared `base` image getting them too. `pkg install` had exactly one destination, hardcoded (`pkg_build_completed()`'s merge step, `daemon/src/pkg.c`). See ADR-0020 for the full design.

`pkg_install_start()`/`pkg_get_one()`/`pkg_delete()` all gained an `image` parameter (`NULL`/empty defaults to `"base"` — every existing caller is unaffected). Because the same package name is now independently trackable per target image, the registry's lookup key became a **(name, image) compound key** — `struct pkg_entry` gained an `image[]` field, persisted in `pkg_installed.json` (absent on load defaults to `"base"`, so every pre-existing state file stays valid). A package's own dependency chain always installs into the same target image as the top-level request. `POST /v1/pkg/install`'s body gained an optional `"image"` field; the two single-resource endpoints gained `@`-separated compound addressing, `GET`/`DELETE /v1/pkg/{name}@{image}` (bare `{name}` still means `base`), reusing the existing path segment rather than adding query-string parsing (confirmed entirely absent from `http.h`). CLI: `pkg install --image=NAME`, `pkg rm NAME[@IMAGE]` (the latter needed no code change — it already forwards its argument straight into the URL path), `pkg ls` gained an image column.

Verified end-to-end over real HTTP against a live daemon (`test/test_pkg.c`, extended): the same recipe (`greeter`) installed into both `base` and `router` lands in two genuinely separate rootfs trees with zero cross-contamination, `GET /v1/pkg` lists both as distinct entries, `@`-addressed `GET`/`DELETE` reach the right one, and deleting `greeter@router` leaves the independently-installed `greeter@base` untouched — 3 consecutive clean runs. Full regression sweep (`test_daemon`, `test_cli`, `test_networks`, `test_dns`, `test_pki`, `test_daemon_net`, `test_daemon_devices`) re-run clean against the rebuilt daemon.

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** no image *creation* endpoint — a target rootfs is created on demand by the first install into it, the same way `base` itself already behaves before `pkg bootstrap` (closed in part 8's own follow-up: C-runtime seeding is now generalized to every image, see ADR-0023); v1's single-install-in-flight-at-a-time serialization is still global across every image, not per-image (a `router` install still 409s while a `base` install is mid-flight) — a documented, not-yet-relaxed boundary.

### Phase 12 part 6 (done): explicit, operator-chosen IPs for container network attachments

Also from the router use case: a router's own interfaces typically need stable, predictable addresses, not whatever `network_alloc_ip()`'s first-free-address scan happens to pick. See ADR-0021.

Each `networks[]` array entry in `POST /v1/containers` may now be an object, `{"name": "...", "ip": "..."}`, instead of only a bare name — an explicit address is validated against the target network's own subnet (correct masked base, not the reserved gateway or network address, within the usable host range) and checked for collision against every other running container's attachments, then honored exactly as given rather than auto-allocated. `daemon/src/registry.c` gained `registry_ip_available()`, a pure collision check extracted from `registry_alloc_ip()`'s own scan loop (registry.c stays deliberately topology-agnostic, per its own existing design); `daemon/src/network.c` gained `network_ip_available()`, which owns the subnet/range/gateway validation and delegates the final collision check to the registry. CLI: `--network=NAME:IP`, parsed the same way `--route=DEST/PREFIX:VIA` already is.

Verified over real HTTP against a live daemon: `test/test_networks.c` extended to confirm an explicit valid IP is honored (not auto-allocated), and that the reserved gateway address, an address outside the subnet, and an already-assigned address are each correctly rejected (400/400/409) — 3 consecutive clean runs. Full regression sweep re-run clean.

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** two network attachments in the *same* `POST /v1/containers` request aren't cross-checked against each other for an IP collision, only against already-registered containers — mirrors a pre-existing boundary `network_alloc_ip()` itself already had, not a new gap, and not a realistic use case (attaching to the same network twice in one request).

### Phase 12 part 7 (done): real network interface passthrough

The third and largest piece of the router use case: a real PCI/USB NIC has no `/dev` node at all, so the existing device-passthrough mechanism (BPF_CGROUP_DEVICE, ADR-0017) doesn't apply — the only real kernel primitive is moving the interface's netdev into a container's own network namespace. See ADR-0022 for the full account, including two real bugs found and fixed only while building this part's own verification: the kernel administratively downs a link as part of moving it to a new netns (the original "bring it up before moving" design was wrong, corrected to bring it up after, from inside the target netns), and a first draft of the verification test itself was fooled by `/sys/class/net`'s mount-time-captured (not dynamically netns-aware) view.

`daemon/src/device.c` gained a third bus, `net:`, walking `/sys/class/net` and excluding every kernel-created software interface (bridges, veths, kanxeo's own managed networks — anything resolving under `/sys/devices/virtual/net/`) — a more precise discriminator than a name-based heuristic. `src/container_net.c` gained `container_net_host_attach_interfaces()` (parent side, moves the interface in, then a forked helper briefly enters the container's own netns to bring it up) and `container_net_teardown_interfaces()` (a forked helper does the reverse move at container removal, addressed via an fd on `/proc/<pid>/ns/net` held open for the interface's entire attachment lifetime specifically so a self-exiting container doesn't lose it to the kernel's own unrecoverable fallback-name path). `netplane/src/rtnetlink.c` gained `rtnl_link_set_netns_fd()` (moves a link via an open netns fd instead of a pid, needed since the owning process may already be gone by teardown time). `POST /v1/containers` gained an `"interfaces": ["wlan0"]` array, validated against `GET /v1/devices`' own `net:` entries; CLI gained repeatable `run --interface=IFNAME`.

Verified two ways: `GET /v1/devices`' new discovery logic was checked directly against this dev sandbox's own real hardware (a real PCI NIC present in `/sys/bus/pci/devices` but, confirmed directly, invisible in this LXC's own `/sys/class/net` — a real, documented sandbox limitation, not a bug); the actual netns-move mechanism was proven end-to-end in `test/test_container_net.c` using a real veth pair's own two ends as a stand-in for "a named interface not yet in a container's netns" (legitimate since `rtnl_link_set_netns_pid()`/`rtnl_link_set_netns_fd()` are confirmed fully generic, not veth-specific) — 3 consecutive clean runs, full regression sweep (every other test in the suite) re-run clean.

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** genuine end-to-end proof with a literal physical NIC handed to a literal container has not happened and can't in this sandbox (no real, physically-backed netdev is visible in its own root netns at all) — real deployment hardware, or a future QEMU boot harness with a passed-through NIC, would be needed for that; the mechanism itself is proven, the literal hardware path is an explicit, known-open item, not silently assumed to work. Part 9's own follow-up closed the boundary precisely: `test/test_daemon_devices.c` now proves what *is* testable without real hardware (a real veth never appears under `GET /v1/devices`' `bus: "net"`, and `POST /v1/containers` correctly rejects it as an interface) — negative/validation paths only, the positive "grant a real NIC" path remains the open item above.

### Phase 12 part 8 (done): C-runtime seeding generalized to every image

ADR-0019's own Consequences section named this gap directly: runtime seeding (`ld.so`/`libc.so.6`/`libtinfo.so.6`) only ever landed in `images/base/rootfs`, at real install time — a `router` image with `bird`/`bash`/`iproute2` installed into it (part 5's own per-image `pkg install`) had no way to execve() any of them. See ADR-0023.

Investigating a runtime-level fix surfaced a real, previously unexamined risk first: the natural design (mirroring `pkg_bootstrap_build_image()`'s own "copy from wherever kanxeod is running" precedent) would have silently found nothing to copy for `libtinfo.so.6` on any real deploy, since `image/src/mkbootroot.c`'s own control-plane squashfs only staged `ld.so`/`libc.so.6` before this change — working in this dev sandbox only by coincidence (a rich `/usr` this sandbox happens to have, not something Kanxeo's own architecture guaranteed). Fixed first: `mkbootroot.c` now also stages `libtinfo.so.6`, so the *running* system's own root reliably carries all three files on any real deploy.

`daemon/src/pkg.c`/`daemon/include/pkg.h` gained `pkg_seed_image_runtime(const char *image)` — copies the three runtime files into any image's rootfs from those same fixed host paths, idempotent, tolerant of a missing source file (skip, not fatal, matching `pkg_bootstrap_build_image()`'s own precedent). Called from `pkg_build_completed()` for whatever image a job merges into, `base` included (harmlessly redundant there). A genuine addition, not a supersession of ADR-0019 — `base`'s own install-time mechanism is untouched.

Verified: `test/test_pkg.c`'s existing per-image scenario extended to confirm all three runtime files land in a freshly created `router` image's rootfs after its first install — 3 consecutive clean runs. `kanxeo-install.c`/`mkinstalleriso.c` confirmed byte-for-byte untouched (zero diff); a full QEMU-level `test_installer.c` re-run was not performed this session (no kernel image cached, and the change never touches that code path) — noted explicitly as a low-risk, known gap rather than silently skipped.

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** on a real deploy whose running root genuinely lacks these three files for some other reason, seeding still silently no-ops (same tolerant-of-missing-source rule); `pkg_bootstrap_build_image()`'s own toolchain sourcing has the identical "copy from wherever kanxeod is running" shape and was not fixed here — a real installed system's minimal squashfs root likely still lacks a full build toolchain for `pkg bootstrap` itself, a real, separate, pre-existing gap confirmed while investigating this one but explicitly out of scope for this change.

### Phase 12 part 9 (done): image lifecycle endpoints (`GET`/`POST`/`DELETE /v1/images`)

Before this part, an image was purely implicit — a directory that came into existence either at real install time (`base`) or as a side effect of the first `pkg install` targeting a name never seen before. No way to list what images exist, create one explicitly, or delete one. See ADR-0024.

New `daemon/src/image.c`/`daemon/include/image.h`, mirroring `/networks`' own four-endpoint shape but deliberately **not** persisted — an image genuinely is its directory, re-enumerated fresh on every list/get call, the same posture `device.c` already has for host hardware. `POST /images` creates an empty rootfs and immediately seeds its C runtime (part 8's own `pkg_seed_image_runtime()`), so it's usable right away. `DELETE /images/{name}` refuses `"base"` outright (protected, never removable), refuses if a running container still references it (`registry_image_in_use()`, `registry_entry` gaining a `char image[]` field via a new explicit parameter on `registry_create()` — `GET /v1/containers` responses gain an `"image"` field as a natural side effect), and refuses if `pkg.c` still tracks any package against it (`pkg_image_has_packages()`) — avoiding orphaned `pkg.c` state referencing a rootfs that no longer exists. Recursive removal is a hand-rolled `nftw()`-based walk, not a shell-out to `rm -rf`, consistent with this project's own "hand-rolled C for daemon-owned operations" posture. CLI: `image create --name=NAME`, `image ls`, `image rm NAME`.

Verified over real HTTP against a live daemon: `test/test_images.c`, new — create/list/get, 409 on duplicate create, runtime seeded immediately after create, 400 deleting `base`, 404 for an unknown image, 409 deleting an image a running container references (then 204 once that container is gone, directory genuinely removed), 409 deleting an image with a package still tracked against it (a deliberately-wrong-checksum install, reaching FAILED fast with no real build ever launching, but still counted as "tracked" — exactly the property under test). 3 consecutive clean runs. Full regression sweep re-run clean.

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** no image rename or clone — `router` still means starting empty and `pkg install`ing into it, not forking `base`'s own contents; not a v1 need identified yet.

## Phase 13: container lifecycle durability

A distinct theme from Phase 12's device/network/package work: containers themselves have been in-memory only since Phase 3 (deliberate at the time — every container dies via `PR_SET_PDEATHSIG` if the daemon exits, no restart-orphan state to reconcile). For a real deployment (routers, a NAS, a git-repo container, an ad-blocking DNS server, some depending on others), that boundary became the real blocker, raised directly by the user.

### Phase 13 part 1 (done): `restart: "always"` + `depends_on`
