A second, genuinely pre-existing bug surfaced independently while tracing why a freshly-autostarted container still reported `stopped: true` despite visibly running: `containerdef_autostart_all()` already correctly *ignores* a stale `stopped=1` flag when deciding whether to autostart a `"always"`/`"on-failure"` container (ADR-0027's own documented behavior -- a daemon restart is a fresh chance regardless of a prior manual stop) -- but it never actually *cleared* that flag on a successful autostart. `handle_restart_timer_event()` (the crash-restart path) checks `!def->stopped` **unconditionally, regardless of policy** -- so any container manually stopped even once, then revived by one daemon restart, would come back live but permanently ineligible for its own declared restart policy from that point on, with no visible symptom anywhere except a missing restart after some future, unrelated crash. Fixed with the same one-line `containerdef_set_stopped(name, 0)` call `handle_start()` already makes on its own success path.

Making `start` actually *reachable* forced a third, related fix: for a stopped container to be findable at all (from the CLI, the web dashboard, or a bare `GET`), "not in the live registry" had to stop meaning "doesn't exist." New `containerdef_write_json_stopped_list()`/`containerdef_write_json_stopped_one()` (`daemon/src/containerdef.c`) synthesize a minimal, correctly-shaped `Container` JSON entry (status `"stopped"`, `image` recovered by a read-only parse of the persisted request body's own `"image"` field, every field a live entry has but a stopped one doesn't left `null`/empty) for any definition with `stopped == 1` -- now a safe, true invariant given the autostart fix above (no live-registry cross-check needed). `registry_write_json_list()` and `handle_get_one()` both fall back to this. Fixing this against a real, already-running instance of this project's own live demo topology caught one more thing directly: the `blah`→`srv1` VRRP round-trip had actually been silently broken this whole time (100% ping loss) because `srv1` was missing its own static route back to `lan1` via the VRRP address -- exactly the asymmetric-routing bug Phase 24's own writeup already described and had supposedly fixed, but a subsequent state-recovery recreation of these containers (mid-session, off the record) had dropped the fix. Recreated correctly and re-verified: 0% loss, full VRRP failover intact.

Extended everywhere this project's own conventions require: `docs/api/openapi.yaml` (three new paths, `Container.status` gains `"paused"`, a new `paused: boolean` field), `kanxeoctl start/pause/unpause NAME`, and the web dashboard (buttons gated on live status, a right-click context menu, a distinct amber tree-status dot for paused, a real `--paused`/`--paused-bg` color pair). New `test/test_container_lifecycle.c` proves all of it against a real, isolated `kanxeod` subprocess (`--data-dir=`, ADR-0044): stop-then-still-visible, start-without-a-daemon-restart, a real cgroup-freeze/thaw checked against `cgroup.events` directly (not just the REST status string), double-pause/double-unpause both correctly 409 (not idempotent-200, unlike start/stop -- a caller should already know its own last-observed pause state), pause-then-delete completing in under a second (not hanging), and the autostart stale-flag fix confirmed across a real daemon restart. Fixing this also broke two existing assertions in `test_container_restart.c` that had (correctly, before this phase) relied on "`GET` returns 404" as a proxy for "not live" -- both helpers (`fetch_pid()`, `container_exists()`) updated to check the `status` field instead, restoring their real original intent rather than the literal HTTP code.

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** no terminal `resize`/`SIGWINCH` propagation for pause/unpause specifically (not applicable -- that gap belongs to the console feature, already named there). Pause/unpause do not persist across a daemon restart (a paused container's process dies with the daemon like every other container, per this project's own stated in-memory-only design) -- an operator who pauses something and then restarts the daemon gets it back running, not still paused; not treated as a bug, matching the existing "no restart-orphan state to reconcile" design this whole registry already commits to.

## Phase 35 (done): site-scoped DNS/PKI naming + a real intermediate CA (ADR-0046, ADR-0047)

Raised directly by the user in the same batch of platform-maturity questions Phase 34 came from: should a root CA's certs live under a domain, and should there be an intermediate CA tier? Investigated first: DNS records and PKI leaf SANs have always been bare, unvalidated operator-supplied strings (`dns_name_is_valid()`'s own label rules, nothing more), and PKI (`daemon/src/pki.c`) has only ever had one self-signed root signing every leaf directly. The obvious default (hardcode `.internal`) was explicitly rejected: "this should be configurable, and the site name a configurable... this has an effect on both DNS and PKI, and should have the necessary API" -- confirmed via `AskUserQuestion` before writing any code.

**Site config** (new `daemon/src/siteconfig.c`/`.h`, ADR-0046): a real, minimal, persisted `site_name`/`domain_suffix` pair (`domain_suffix` defaults to `"internal"`, IANA-reserved for exactly this use per RFC 9476, but is genuinely settable -- `site_name` may be `""`, meaning no site tier, a single-site deployment). New `GET`/`PUT /v1/system/site` -- the platform's first `PUT` endpoint (every prior mutating endpoint used `POST`; a real "replace this resource's current state" semantic this one actually fits). A deliberate, explicit scope boundary, stated up front rather than assumed, **as of this phase**: this is a *convenience*, never enforcement -- no daemon-side code reads `site_name`/`domain_suffix` to validate or qualify a DNS record or PKI SAN; the only consumer is the web dashboard's own site-settings form, which composes a suggested FQDN (`<name>.<site_name>.<domain_suffix>`) entirely client-side for an operator to edit or accept. (A later phase, Phase 38/ADR-0052 below, adds real server-side default qualification for a bare name via `siteconfig_qualify()` -- this Phase 35 boundary describes the system as it stood at the time, not as it stands after that later change.) Retroactively enforcing a suffix was considered and explicitly rejected -- this sandbox's own live demo topology (`cr-1`, `srv1`, `blah`, ...) has always used short, unqualified names and keeps working completely unchanged.

**Intermediate CA** (`pki_intermediate_create()`, ADR-0047): a real second keypair and cert, signed *by* the root (never self-signed) via the same `-addext`-at-CSR-time-then-`-copy_extensions`-at-signing pattern leaf issuance already established -- `basicConstraints=critical,CA:TRUE,pathlen:0` (capped at exactly one extra tier, matching the real need, not a speculative arbitrary depth) and `keyUsage=critical,keyCertSign,cRLSign`. One-shot, mirroring the root's own `pki_ca_create()` precedent exactly (`PKI_ERR_NOT_BOOTSTRAPPED` if the root doesn't exist yet, `PKI_ERR_ALREADY_BOOTSTRAPPED` on a second call -- with endpoint-specific error wording, since the generic mapper's root-CA phrasing would otherwise be actively misleading here). `pki_cert_create()`'s own signing step is unchanged at the API level -- internally it now signs with the intermediate whenever one exists, the root directly otherwise, fully transparent and backward-compatible: every existing `--pki-issue` container flow and every already-issued leaf keeps working unmodified. `pki_cert_deliver()` now writes the real, complete chain (leaf + intermediate, standard `fullchain.pem` order) into a running container's `tls.crt` when an intermediate exists.

Verified as real cryptography throughout, not string/status checks: `openssl verify -CAfile root.crt intermediate.crt` → `OK` (the intermediate genuinely chains to the root); a freshly issued leaf's own `openssl x509 -noout -issuer` shows the intermediate's CN, not the root's; that same leaf explicitly does **not** verify against the root alone (`openssl verify -CAfile root.crt leaf.crt` correctly fails with "unable to get local issuer certificate") but **does** verify once the intermediate completes the chain (`openssl verify -CAfile root.crt -untrusted intermediate.crt leaf.crt` → `OK`) -- proving the leaf was actually signed by the intermediate, not just claimed to be. A real running container's own delivered `/etc/kanxeo-tls/tls.crt` was confirmed to genuinely contain 2 certificates in the correct leaf-then-intermediate order via `openssl pkcs7 -print_certs`.

New endpoints (`GET`/`POST /v1/pki/intermediate`, `GET`/`PUT /v1/system/site`) extended everywhere this project's own conventions require: `docs/api/openapi.yaml` (new `SiteConfig`/`PkiIntermediateCreateRequest` schemas), `kanxeoctl site show`/`site set`/`pki intermediate bootstrap`/`pki intermediate show`, and the web dashboard (a Site settings form under System, an Intermediate CA block under PKI, both cross-checked against the served `index.html`/`app.js` for matching DOM ids -- no browser in this sandbox, the same honest boundary every prior dashboard phase has stated). `test/test_pki.c` extended with real end-to-end coverage for both (Part 3: intermediate bootstrap + double-bootstrap 409 + the full chain-verification proof above; Part 4: site config defaults + PUT round-trip + invalid-suffix 400) -- reusing the same already-real-daemon-restart this test's own persistence check already performs, rather than paying for a second one, since `siteconfig.c`'s persistence is the identical `persist_atomic_write()`/`persist_read_file()` primitive already independently proven elsewhere. Full clean rebuild, zero warnings; broader regression sweep (18 daemon-linked tests) re-run clean.

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** no rotation/renewal mechanism for the intermediate (same accepted boundary the root has always had). No REST-level "fetch the assembled chain for cert X" endpoint -- only the *delivered*, on-disk `tls.crt` a running container actually gets carries the full chain; `GET /v1/pki/certs/{name}` still reports leaf-only `cert_pem`, unchanged shape, since nothing asked for more. Multi-host/multi-site naming patterns (`<host>.<site>.internal` across a real fleet) remain a real, named-but-undesigned future need -- this phase covers one install's own declared identity, not a cross-host naming scheme.

**Follow-up, directly from the user against the live dashboard**: two real gaps closed in `web/app.js` alone, no daemon change -- the Site settings form was clobbered by its own poll (`refreshSiteConfig()` overwrote whatever was mid-typed every ~2s, reported as "I cannot change the site/domain"; fixed with the same dirty-tracking guard Phase 37's own recipe editor already established), and the client-side FQDN suggestion this phase's own text above already claimed ("client tooling can suggest a default FQDN") was in fact never wired into the DNS-record/PKI-cert forms -- now real: a bare label auto-expands to `<name>.<site_name>.<domain_suffix>` on blur, still freely editable.

**Second follow-up**: "would giving the kanxeo instance a name make sense, so it can be managed?" -- `instance_name` joins `site_name`/`domain_suffix` as a third field on the same subsystem (not a new one), always non-empty, defaults to `"kanxeo"`. Surfaced in the dashboard header/tab title, `kanxeoctl site show`, and -- closing a real pre-existing gap -- the backup/restore bundle, which never carried site config at all before this. `kanxeoctl site set` now fetches-then-merges instead of a blind single-shot PUT, fixing a latent footgun where setting just one field silently blanked the others. `test/test_pki.c` Part 4 extended (instance_name defaults/round-trip/validation); `test/test_system_backup.c` extended to prove `site_config` actually round-trips through the bundle. No ADR -- additive to an already-decided subsystem, not a new hard-to-reverse call.

## Phase 36 (done): persistent device name mappings, Proxmox-style (ADR-0048)

Raised directly by the user, against a concrete real-world precedent: "can we make each device an item under the devices tree only if we've named it? Naming it will work exactly like Proxmox's device mapping -- specific, or vendor/model, since then we can move it around on USB ports." Investigated first: host hardware discovery (`daemon/src/device.c`) is deliberately never persisted -- re-enumerated fresh from sysfs every call, since this is hardware the daemon doesn't own or create. Correct for discovery, but left nothing an operator could build a stable container spec around: a USB device's own `id` bakes in either its real serial or, absent one, its physical port path -- a container referencing it directly either breaks the moment the device moves to a different port, or has to be pinned to one exact bus location forever.

New `daemon/src/devicemap.c`/`.h`: a real, persisted `{name, kind, selector}` layer on top of discovery, both kinds explicitly requested by the user rather than picked unilaterally -- `"exact"` (selector is a real device id, pinned to one bus location) and `"vendor_model"` (selector is `"<vendor_id>:<product_id>"`, resolves regardless of port -- the actual "move it around" mechanism). Resolution is never cached, mirroring `device_find()`/`device_find_group()`'s own existing "always re-derive, host hardware can change" precedent exactly; a `vendor_model` mapping matching more than one currently-plugged-in device grants all of them, reusing GPU passthrough's own existing group-expansion behavior (ADR-0028) rather than inventing new ambiguity handling. A mapping is real and creatable even for hardware that isn't currently present -- `GET /v1/devicemaps` reports `present`/`resolved_ids` fresh on every call rather than refusing the mapping's own existence.

Container integration: `main.c`'s existing `devices[]` parsing now tries a mapping-name resolution first, falling back to the pre-existing raw-id path only when the name isn't a mapping at all -- fully backward-compatible, confirmed directly (a raw device id still works exactly as before). A real, deliberate asymmetry: a mapping that *exists* but currently resolves to nothing is a 400, not silently retried as a literal raw id -- conflating "not a mapping" with "mapped device is absent" would mask a real error in one direction or shadow an unrelated coincidentally-identical id in the other.

Web dashboard: the Devices tree category, previously a flat link with zero children, now lists one child per *named* mapping only, exactly as asked -- raw unnamed hardware never appears in the tree. The Devices view itself gained a tab-bar split by bus (USB / PCI / Network / GPU, reusing Phase 32's own `.tab-bar`/`.tab-panel[data-tab]` pattern verbatim -- the page's one generic tab-switching handler picked up the new tabs with zero new JS) plus a "Named mappings" table showing live present/absent status per mapping. A "Name…" button on each unmapped device row opens the shared modal, pre-filled with that device's own id as the default exact selector.

Verified end-to-end against this sandbox's own real USB hardware (a Western Digital Game Drive, confirmed via `GET /v1/devices`): created an exact mapping, confirmed a container referencing the mapping *name* resolved to the real device id; created a vendor/model mapping from the same device's own vendor:product pair, confirmed it resolved the identical real id; confirmed a mapping pointing at a deliberately nonexistent selector correctly 400s at container-creation time rather than silently falling back to raw-id interpretation; confirmed the raw-id fallback path itself still works unchanged. `test/test_daemon_devices.c` extended with all of the above as new, permanent, hardware-adaptive test coverage (skipped, not failed, on a host with no assignable device at all -- same precedent the file's own pre-existing scenario 3 already established). Full clean rebuild, zero warnings; 19-test regression sweep re-run clean.

## Phase 37 (done): Packages tree UI -- a recipe tab and an installed-versions tab per package

The last of the batch of platform-maturity questions Phases 34-36 came from: "under packages tree, can we list the packages with a tab (per package) for the recipe itself, to show it and possibly edit it, and versions of it -- and another tab where it's installed, with versions?" A pure UI/UX layer over data that already fully existed (`pkg_write_json_recipes()`, `pkg_write_json_list()`), matching Phase 32's own precedent (the image detail tabs were the same kind of change -- no new backend concept, just a better view onto one) -- no ADR, same reasoning.

One real backend gap turned up during investigation, not assumed going in: `pkg_write_json_recipes()`'s list shape (`name`/`version`/`depends`) never included a recipe's own raw *content* -- there was no way to read back what was actually uploaded short of shelling into the host filesystem. New `pkg_recipe_get()` (`daemon/src/pkg.c`) fills that one gap, mirroring `pki_ca_get()`'s own "write straight into a `json_writer`" shape rather than inventing a separate content-only accessor. `GET /v1/pkg/recipes/{name}` (`daemon/include/pkg.h`, new `PkgRecipeDetail` schema in `docs/api/openapi.yaml`) returns `{name, version, depends, content}` -- 404 for an unknown name, the same "no fallback, a name either has a recipe on file or it doesn't" contract `DELETE` already established. `kanxeoctl pkg recipe show NAME` prints the raw content directly, matching the pattern the file-upload-based `pkg recipe add` already set.

Web dashboard: the Packages tree category, previously two static links ("Recipes"/"Installed"), now lists one child per package *name* -- the union of every recipe on file and every name tracked in `GET /v1/pkg` (a recipe can be deleted without touching what it already installed, ADR-0040, so the two sets legitimately diverge; neither list alone is the right answer). A new landing table (`#view-packages`) shows every name with its recipe version and installed-image count, each linking into a per-package detail view (`#view-package-detail`) with the same `.tab-bar`/`.tab-panel[data-tab]` pattern Phase 32/36 already established -- **Recipe** (version/depends fields, the raw content in a scrollable `<pre>`, "Edit…"/"Remove recipe" actions) and **Installed** (image/version/state/available/error/files, per-row Remove, reusing the existing `DELETE /v1/pkg/{name@image}` path). A package with installed history but no recipe on file (deleted independently) shows an explicit "no recipe on file" state instead of an empty/broken tab. "Edit…" reuses the existing recipe-upload modal rather than a new one -- the modal gained an optional `<textarea>` alongside its existing file input (whichever is non-empty at submit time wins), letting the same single `POST /v1/pkg/recipes` upsert serve both "browse a local .recipe file" (unchanged) and "edit fetched content in place" (new) without a second form or a second code path. Recipe content is fetched once per name and cached client-side, not re-fetched on every 2s poll tick -- the same guard `openConsole()` already uses for its own per-container WebSocket, so a poll-driven re-render of the same detail page never clobbers an in-progress edit.

`test/test_pkg.c` extended: `GET /v1/pkg/recipes/{name}` returns the exact content just upserted (byte-for-byte, not just a status check), 404 for a name that was never added, and 404 again after that same recipe is deleted (confirming the recipe is genuinely gone, not just uninstalled). Full clean rebuild, zero warnings; regression sweep re-run clean under the correct privileged invocation (`sudo` + the Bash tool's sandbox disabled -- see the `CLAUDE.md` environment note this same investigation added in Phase 36).

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** no inline syntax highlighting or validation-before-save in the Recipe tab's edit flow -- `pkg_recipe_add()`'s own existing parse-and-validate-before-write already rejects a bad edit server-side (400, nothing written), just not previewed client-side first. No diff view between a recipe's on-disk content and an installed package's own recorded version (the `available_version` drift field already surfaces *that* a recipe has moved on, not what changed).

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** no per-mapping detail view in the web dashboard (clicking a named device in the tree shows the same Devices view the Named mappings table already lives in, not a dedicated single-mapping page) -- a real, honest scope boundary, not a missing feature in practice since the table is right there. No mapping-name uniqueness enforcement across kinds (an operator could name the same physical device twice under two different mappings, once exact and once vendor/model) -- not prevented, since there's no general way to know two selectors refer to "the same" device, and no real harm from allowing it.

## Phase 38 (done): a real platform identity -- domain-based CA naming, an auto-issued host cert, image trust, default name qualification, an auto-maintained DNS record, a real management DNS container (ADR-0049 through ADR-0053)

Direct follow-up to `instance_name` (itself a follow-up to Phase 35): the root/intermediate CA's common names should reflect this install's own `domain_suffix`, not a fixed placeholder ("Test Root CA" on this project's own live daemon, bootstrapped in an earlier phase, with a real intermediate and at least one real leaf -- the lldap container's cert, Phase 25 -- already issued under it). `pki_ca_create()`/`pki_intermediate_create()` are one-shot by design (`PKI_ERR_ALREADY_BOOTSTRAPPED` on a second call) -- there was no way at all to change an already-bootstrapped CA's subject. Confirmed with the user via `AskUserQuestion` before writing any code: regenerate now, reissuing every leaf currently tracked, rather than leaving them orphaned or deferring the rename to a future reset-from-empty.

New `pki_ca_reset()` (`daemon/src/pki.c`, ADR-0049): snapshots every tracked leaf, wipes root/intermediate/leaf key+cert files and the now-stale index, re-bootstraps root (and intermediate, only if one existed before the call) via the existing one-shot functions, then reissues every snapshotted leaf -- same name/SANs/owner, a fresh keypair, `leaf_days` validity. Every reissued leaf's `cert_pem` **and** `key_pem` are included in the response -- a CA reset is a genuine new issuance moment for each, so it gets the identical "shown exactly once, right now" treatment a leaf's key already gets at first issuance, rather than silently losing it for any leaf with no live owner to redeliver into. New `POST /v1/pki/reset` (`root_common_name`/`intermediate_common_name`/`root_days`/`intermediate_days`/`leaf_days`, all optional, defaulting to `"Kanxeo Root/Intermediate CA - <domain_suffix>"` composed from the current site config via a new minimal `siteconfig_domain_suffix()` accessor). `kanxeoctl pki reset` (no confirmation flag, matching every other destructive `kanxeoctl` subcommand's own direct-execution convention); the web dashboard gets a clearly-labeled destructive action under PKI, gated behind a `confirm()` dialog matching `reboot`/`shutdown`'s own existing pattern.

Redelivering a reissued leaf into any still-live container that owns it (so a running service's `tls.crt`/`tls.key` don't go stale) surfaced a real, independent gap along the way, caught by verification rather than assumed away: the first implementation enumerated live containers via `containerdef_resolve_order()`, mirroring `containerdef_autostart_all()`'s own idiom -- but `containerdef_add()` only persists a definition for `restart != "no"`, so a perfectly ordinary `restart:"no"` container with `pki_issue:true` has no containerdef entry to enumerate through at all, and was silently never redelivered. Caught by comparing a live test container's own delivered cert file content before and after reset, not just checking the reset endpoint's status code. Fixed with a new `registry_list_names()` (`daemon/src/registry.c`) enumerating every live registry entry regardless of persistence, and a new `pki_cert_owned_by()` (`daemon/src/pki.c`) checking ownership against the PKI index itself rather than re-parsing a request body that may not exist.

`test/test_pki.c` Part 5 extended with real end-to-end proof, not just response-shape checks: a leaf's old cert genuinely stops verifying against the new root (`openssl verify` fails post-reset, confirmed as a real failure not a skipped check), the reissued leaf gets a different serial, `GET` reflects the new serial afterward (not a stale index entry), and a live `pki_issue`-owning container's own on-disk `tls.crt` is confirmed byte-different after the reset (the actual redelivery proof). Verified against this project's own live production daemon too: reset the real root/intermediate (now `CN = Kanxeo Root CA - home.arpa` / `CN = Kanxeo Intermediate CA - home.arpa`), confirmed the `cr-1`/`cr-2`/`srv1`/`blah` demo topology unaffected (different image, no PKI dependency) with a real `nsenter`+`ping` end-to-end check. Full clean rebuild, zero warnings; full 17-test daemon-linked regression sweep re-run clean.

One CLI bug caught only by testing the actual command, not just the endpoint it calls: `cmd_pki_reset()`'s own `strncmp()` prefix-length constants for `--root-common-name=`/`--intermediate-common-name=`/`--intermediate-days=` were each off by one (19/27/20 characters, not the 20/28/21 first written) -- every value-bearing invocation of those three flags silently fell through to "unknown option" while the raw REST endpoint itself worked correctly the whole time. Fixed and reverified with real flag values against the live daemon.

**Part 2 (ADR-0050): an auto-issued "host" leaf, always in sync with site config.** "By default we have a Cert for host.site.domain_suffix?" -- the leaf a future real-TLS-on-`kanxeod`-itself phase (not part of this work, deliberately deferred with its own fresh planning pass when it starts) will eventually serve, built now so it already exists and is correct by the time that lands. New `siteconfig_host_fqdn()` (`daemon/src/siteconfig.c`/`.h`) composes `<instance_name>.<site_name>.<domain_suffix>`. New `reissue_host_pki_cert()` (`daemon/src/main.c`): a fixed record name (`"host"`, never the FQDN itself -- a rename via `PUT /v1/system/site` would otherwise orphan one differently-named cert per change) with the real, current FQDN as its sole SAN; delete-then-recreate keeps exactly one `"host"` leaf ever tracked. Called from every point the host's identity could have just changed or newly become expressible: `POST /pki/ca`, `POST /pki/intermediate`, `PUT /system/site`, and `POST /pki/reset` -- best-effort throughout, a missing root CA (the common state on a fresh install before PKI is ever touched) is logged, never surfaced as a failure on the actually-requested operation. `test/test_pki.c` Part 4 extended: after a site PUT, `GET /pki/certs/host` carries the just-set FQDN in its SAN with `owner: null`; changing `instance_name` again gets a genuinely different serial (real reissue, not a stale index entry). Verified live: `gibsson.uk.home.arpa` now exists as a real, intermediate-signed leaf on this project's own production daemon.

**Part 3 (ADR-0051): the CA trust chain staged into every image.** "We also make sure that our root and intermediate are installed on any of our images... so that we are trusted by our containers." Investigated first, not assumed: no image this platform has ever built shipped any CA trust at all, not even public roots -- every TLS client inside a container fails verification outright today without `-k`/an explicit `--cacert`. New `pki_write_trust_bundle_file()` (`daemon/src/pki.c`/`.h`): root, plus the intermediate's if bootstrapped, concatenated to a destination path -- a different job from `pki_cert_deliver()`'s own leaf-plus-intermediate `fullchain.pem` (order doesn't matter for a pure trust-anchor bundle the way it does there). `pkg_seed_image_baseline()` (`daemon/src/pkg.c`) gains a fourth block writing `etc/ssl/certs/kanxeo-ca-bundle.pem` into every new image -- idempotent, and tolerant of no CA existing yet (the common state on a fresh install's first-ever image), same posture the existing runtime-libs/dev-nodes blocks already established for their own respective tolerances. `test/test_pki.c` Part 6: creates a real image via `POST /v1/images`, reads the bundle straight off disk, and proves it's a genuine working trust anchor -- `openssl verify -CAfile <bundle> host.crt` → `OK` -- not just "the file exists." This project's own `base`/`router`/`dev`/`pkgbuild` images (created before this feature existed) manually reseeded once during verification, confirmed against the live production daemon with a real `openssl verify` (a first manual attempt, built via naive shell string concatenation, silently corrupted the PEM boundary between root and intermediate -- caught immediately by the same real-verification discipline, not shipped-and-hoped; the actual C code path, proven by the automated test above, was never affected).

**Part 4 (ADR-0052, revises ADR-0046): server-side default DNS/PKI name qualification.** "Since we are doing that, can we make the default DNS entries all postfix with site.domain_suffix? Can be over-ridden?" New `siteconfig_qualify()` (`daemon/src/siteconfig.c`/`.h`): a bare label (no `.`) gets `<label>.<site_name>.<domain_suffix>` appended; a dotted label is returned unchanged -- that *is* the override, no separate flag. Wired into `handle_dns_record_create()`'s `name` and `handle_pki_cert_create()`'s `name` (CN + default SAN, never an explicitly-supplied `sans[]` entry). **Gated on `site_name` being non-empty, not on `domain_suffix` alone -- a real design correction found by running this project's own test suite, not designed that way from the start.** `domain_suffix` defaults to a non-empty `"internal"` (ADR-0046) on every install regardless of configuration; the first version qualified whenever it merely had that default value, which silently qualified every bare name from the very first request on any install, configured or not -- caught immediately when `test_dns.c`'s own bare `"shadow"` record 404'd under its own literal name after becoming `shadow.internal` without anyone asking for that. `site_name` defaults to `""` ("no site tier," already an ADR-0046 concept) and only becomes non-empty once an operator genuinely opts in -- the correct trigger, verified by rerunning the full regression sweep clean afterward. The client-side `suggestedFqdn()` (`web/app.js`) was updated to the identical `site_name` gate in the same pass, closing a matching bug there before it shipped inconsistent with the server. `test/test_dns.c` and `test/test_pki.c` both extended with real qualify/don't-qualify assertions, each restoring `site_name` to `""` afterward so the rest of their own pre-existing bare-name tests keep passing unmodified. Verified live: `POST /dns/records {"name":"testbox"}` against the production daemon (`site_name=uk` already configured) created `testbox.uk.home.arpa` for real.

**Part 5 (ADR-0053): an auto-maintained instance DNS record.** "Wouldn't it make sense to show a dns entry for the hostname... make it editable and it reflects back to the system dns entry?" Confirmed with the user (`AskUserQuestion`): reflects back, not a second independently-editable record -- the identical relationship `reissue_host_pki_cert()` already has with the PKI leaf. New `reconcile_instance_dns_record()` (`daemon/src/main.c`): composes the current FQDN, skips entirely if `g_bind_addr` is `"0.0.0.0"` or `"127.0.0.1"` (no single correct address to publish for either) or isn't a valid IPv4 literal, otherwise deletes whatever it last registered (tracked in-process only via `g_last_instance_dns_name`, not persisted) if the FQDN has changed, then creates the record fresh. Called once at daemon startup (so the record exists without needing a PUT after every restart) and from `handle_site_put()`'s success path. Since every existing test harness in this project binds to the default `127.0.0.1` -- exactly the address this feature treats as "nothing to publish" -- `test/test_dns.c` was extended to start its own daemon instance with a real, specific loopback alias (`--bind=127.0.0.2`, needing no host-side setup since `127.0.0.0/8` is entirely local on Linux) specifically to exercise this path; confirms the default-FQDN record exists at startup with no PUT, and that a live rename deletes the old record and creates the new one (not both, not neither). Not verified against this project's own live production daemon -- it currently runs `--bind=0.0.0.0` (needed for the user's own desktop to reach the dashboard, Phase 35), which this feature correctly and deliberately treats as "no address to publish"; rebinding it to a specific IP just to demonstrate this one feature would have reintroduced the exact connectivity problem that `--bind=0.0.0.0` was chosen to fix, so this was verified through the isolated test above instead, not the live box.

**Part 6: a real, from-source management DNS container.** `daemon/src/dns.c`'s own design has always assumed a real DNS server actually answers queries -- `dns_server_register()` only ever rewrites a registered container's own hosts file and sends `SIGHUP`; nothing in this platform speaks the DNS wire protocol itself. No such server had ever actually been built as an installable package. New `pkg/recipes/dnsmasq.recipe`: Debian's own unmodified `dnsmasq_2.90.orig.tar.xz` (matching this sandbox's own already-verified dnsmasq, the exact binary `test/test_dns.c`'s fixture already proved end-to-end against `dns_server_register()`), a plain `make PREFIX=/usr` with zero runtime dependencies beyond libc (confirmed via `ldd`). Verified for real against the live production daemon, not just planned: uploaded via `POST /pkg/recipes`, installed onto a new `dnssvc` image via `POST /pkg/install`, a `dnsnet` network created with an explicit `--gateway=` (ADR-0037 -- the host needs a real route to `dig` a container directly), a `dns1` container run with the identical proven flags `test/test_dns.c` already established (`-k -u root -g root -p 53 -H /etc/dnsmasq-hosts -R -h -x /etc/dnsmasq.pid`, `/etc/passwd`+`/etc/group` root entries staged via `files`), registered via `POST /dns/servers`, and a real record resolved with the host's own `dig` -- genuine wire-protocol DNS, not a hosts-file check. Explicitly out of scope, per the approved plan: making this reachable from outside Kanxeo's own managed networks -- that needs either enslaving `eth0` (this box's only reachable interface, no recovery path if wrong) or a host-port-forward mechanism this platform doesn't have; this phase proves the container mechanism itself, the same boundary the `lan1`/`wan1` VRRP/OSPF demo topology already drew for routing.

Installing the very first real package onto this `dnssvc` image surfaced a genuine, pre-existing platform bug, not a defect in the new recipe: the build failed with `GNUmakefile:43: /maint.mk: No such file or directory` -- a file dnsmasq's own tarball doesn't contain at all. Traced to the shared `pkgbuild` toolchain image's own persisted rootfs (`images/pkgbuild/rootfs`, the build container's overlay lowerdir on every recipe build) carrying a stale, fully-built `coreutils` source tree at `build/src/` -- almost certainly an uncleaned leftover from Phase 33's own self-hosting toolchain capture. Because the build container's upperdir merges *on top of* that lowerdir, a freshly-reset upperdir extracting dnsmasq's own tarball into `build/src` still exposed the old `GNUmakefile`/`maint.mk` pair from lower, which `make` prefers over dnsmasq's real `Makefile` -- confirmed directly (`sudo find / -iname maint.mk` located the single stray copy, `ls` on it showed a complete, unrelated coreutils checkout, not a partial artifact). Fixed by clearing `build/` from the live `pkgbuild` image's rootfs (confirmed via the user, since it's a destructive action against production daemon state, even though the path itself is pure internal build tooling with no user- or container-facing data). No code path in `daemon/src/pkg.c` writes to this location automatically -- `reset_build_container_dir()` already clears the *container's own* upperdir before every build, exactly as designed -- so this was operational contamination from a past manual step, not a reproducible defect; residual risk is low since the daemon never re-runs the toolchain bootstrap unless an operator explicitly does so, but a future re-bootstrap from the same original squashfs artifact (if it was captured with the same pollution baked in) would need the same cleanup repeated.

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** no rotation/renewal mechanism short of a full reset -- a partial "reissue just this one leaf under a new key, same CA" operation remains unbuilt. `struct pki_cert_record` still has no "signed by which CA tier" field (not needed for this operation). A `restart:"no"` container using both `pki_issue:true` *and* a non-default `--pki-cert-dir=` gets redelivered to the default path after a reset, not its actual custom one -- no persisted body to recover that override from once such a container's creation completes (see ADR-0049's own Consequences). The `"host"` leaf has no consumer yet -- `kanxeod`'s own HTTP listener remains plain HTTP only; nothing manually reserves the `"host"` cert name against an operator choosing it too (see ADR-0050's own Consequences for why this is a narrow, self-inflicted, easily-noticed collision, not worth defending against for a name nobody has an actual reason to pick). A CA reset does not retroactively propagate to any already-staged image's own trust bundle -- a real, named gap (ADR-0051's own Consequences), the image-level analog of the leaf-redelivery problem Phase 38 Part 1 already solves for individual containers. A rename that straddles a daemon restart with no intervening PUT can leave a stale instance DNS record behind (ADR-0053's own Consequences) -- self-heals on the next real rename, not solved proactively. This project's own live production daemon (`--bind=0.0.0.0`) gets no instance DNS record at all today -- a real, current, and correctly-deliberate limitation, not an oversight. The new `dns1` management DNS container is not reachable from outside Kanxeo's own managed networks, and no mechanism to make it so (bridging `eth0`, or host-level port-forwarding) exists yet -- a real, separate, operator-level hypervisor/bridging decision, not attempted here. No automated `test_pkg.c` coverage was added for `dnsmasq.recipe` specifically -- consistent with every other real, from-source recipe in this project (`bird`, `keepalived`, `lldap`, the full `gcc`/`python`/toolchain set): `test_pkg.c` is deliberately network-independent, using only local synthetic fixture tarballs, and every real recipe to date is instead proven live against the actual daemon, exactly as this one was.

## Phase 39 (done): real, host-side per-container stats -- CPU/memory/disk/network, API-first, plus web dashboard graphs (ADR-0054)

Direct user request: out-of-the-box monitoring, per container, pulled entirely from the host side (no in-container agent), exposed through the REST API first ("API driven like everything else"), then graphed in the web dashboard. Researched and empirically verified against this project's own live production daemon before writing any code, not assumed: CPU/memory were already fully available via `struct container_handle.cgroup_fd` (`cpu.stat`/`memory.current`/`memory.stat`/`memory.peak`, already populated for every container); network needed no new rtnetlink code (the host-side veth name is deterministically `vh<pid>-<idx>`, `src/container_net.c`, so `/sys/class/net/<veth>/statistics/*` gives real counters with a plain host-file read); disk space needed a new `nftw()`-based walker but reused `image.c`'s own tree-walk primitive; disk I/O needed the cgroup v2 `io` controller, confirmed not enabled anywhere on this platform. Tested live and reversibly on the production box before committing to the design: enabling it (`echo +io >> cgroup.subtree_control`) succeeded cleanly with zero running containers disrupted and retroactively activated on already-running containers with no recreation needed. Confirmed with the user via `AskUserQuestion`: disk usage means both space consumed and I/O throughput, not one deferred.

New `GET /v1/containers/{name}/stats` (`daemon/src/main.c`'s `handle_container_stats()`): a raw, point-in-time snapshot on every call -- cumulative counters (`cpu.*_usec`, `disk.read/write_bytes/ios`, `networks[].rx/tx_bytes/packets`) or gauges (`memory.current/peak/max`, `disk.upper_bytes`), no server-side history/ring buffer (ADR-0054's central design call -- the daemon stays completely stateless for this feature, "how to graph it" is entirely a client concern). New `src/cgroup.c` readers (`cgroup_read_stat_key()`, `cgroup_read_single_value()`, `cgroup_read_io_totals()`) work directly off the container's already-open `cgroup_fd` via `openat()` -- zero new fd management. New `cgroup_enable_io_accounting()`, best-effort, called once at daemon startup right before `registry_init()`. New `overlay_upperdir_size()` (`src/overlay.c`): an `nftw()` walk summing real file content only, deliberately excluding directory-tree overhead (confirmed live: a container with only empty mountpoint dirs in its own upperdir correctly reports 0 bytes even though `du -sb` on the same path reports several KB of pure directory metadata). Works for a container that exited on its own (`registry_mark_exited()`, cgroup leaf never torn down while the entry exists) but correctly 404s after an explicit `POST .../stop` (`registry_remove()` really does tear the entry down, the same convention `pause`/`unpause` already established).

`kanxeoctl stats NAME` (flat top-level verb, matching `stop`/`start`/`pause`/`unpause`'s own convention, not the `container stats` shape first assumed) prints the raw counters plain-text, one-shot -- no rate/percentage computed CLI-side, that's the web dashboard's own job. `docs/api/openapi.yaml` gets the new path plus a `ContainerStats` schema.

Web dashboard: a 6th "Stats" tab on the container detail view (alongside Summary/Hardware/Options/Console/Backup), four hand-rolled `<canvas>` line charts (CPU/Memory/Disk/Network) -- no charting library, matching this project's own "no external dependencies" posture already established by the console's own terminal renderer and WebSocket codec. `web/app.js` owns a small client-side rolling window (60 samples) kept only while the Stats tab is open, computing CPU% and network KB/s from the delta between consecutive raw samples divided by the real wall-clock time between them (memory/disk stay plain gauges). Polling starts/stops on the same lifecycle points `openConsole()`/`closeConsole()` already use (tab click, container switch, navigating away) -- no separate always-on polling loop.

New `test/test_container_stats.c` (`--data-dir=`-isolated, matching every other daemon-linked test): a real `stats_child.c` fixture that actively burns CPU, touches memory, and appends to a real on-disk file in a loop, proving the returned numbers genuinely advance across two real samples -- not just that the endpoint returns 200 with plausible-looking zeros. Also proves the daemon's own startup-time io-controller enablement takes effect, that `networks[].name` is the network name (never the internal veth name), and both the natural-exit-stays-queryable and stop-really-removes-it claims for real. Two bugs caught only by actually running this new test, not assumed correct from the code alone: `stats_child.c`'s first version burned CPU before writing to disk, so an early sample (well within the test's own polling window) saw genuinely zero disk usage -- fixed by writing first, burning CPU second. The test's own `statsnet` bridge was never explicitly deleted at the end, so a second run of the exact same test collided with its own prior run's leftover bridge on the host (`EEXIST`) -- fixed by adding the same explicit `DELETE /v1/networks/...` cleanup every other network-creating test already does, confirmed idempotent by running the test twice back-to-back.

Full clean rebuild (zero warnings) and full 18-test daemon-linked regression sweep, all passing. Verified live against the production daemon: real, moving CPU/memory/network numbers on a container from the already-running `lan1`/`wan1` demo topology, and byte-exact disk-space accounting (a container's own `upper_bytes` matched the real sum of its staged files' sizes exactly) plus real io-controller-recorded I/O (16384 bytes / 4 IOs matching 4 real file writes) on the `dns1` container. Enabling the `io` controller confirmed to survive a real daemon restart with the entire live topology auto-recovering via `restart:"always"` (the same pattern this project's production daemon has already been restarted through many times this session), no disruption.

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** no real browser click-through of the new Stats tab was possible in this sandboxed dev environment (no headless browser tooling available) -- verified instead via clean JS syntax checks, HTML tag-balance checks, confirming the daemon serves the exact edited files, and a manual dry-run of the identical CPU%/network-rate math against real live API data. A genuine visual/interactive check is still owed the next time this runs somewhere a browser is actually available. The client-side stats history is lost on every page reload or tab re-open (by design, ADR-0054) -- no "last N minutes" view survives a refresh. `kanxeoctl stats` has no `--watch`/live-refreshing mode. No alerting/threshold mechanism exists on top of these numbers -- this phase is visibility only.

## Phase 40 part 1 (done): general container file-read REST endpoint (ADR-0055)

First part of a plan aimed at eventual full self-hosting -- rebuilding both the kernel and Kanxeo's own control plane entirely on-box, no separate dev machine. Designing that surfaced a genuinely missing, independently useful capability first: Kanxeo's `files[]` mechanism (POST /containers, ADR-0030) has always been write-only, host-to-container, staged before `clone3()` -- there was no way to read a file back *out* of a container over the API at all. Confirmed with the user via `AskUserQuestion`: build this as a real, general REST endpoint, not a narrow one-off scoped just to the self-hosting use case.

New `GET /v1/containers/{name}/files?path=...` (`handle_container_file_read()`, `daemon/src/main.c`): the daemon's first query-string-bearing route, needing this project's first query-string parser (`url_query_param()`, deliberately narrow -- one key, `%XX`-only decoding, no repeated-key/array semantics). Path resolution branches on liveness: running (`registry_entry.running == 1`) reads via `/proc/<pid>/root<path>`, the same ADR-0013 magic-symlink pattern `dns.c`/`pki.c`'s own write paths already use, no `setns()`; exited-but-not-removed (`registry_mark_exited()`, entry still present -- only `DELETE` truly removes it, same convention `GET .../stats` already established) reads from the real, host-visible overlay upperdir first, falling back to the image's own read-only rootfs. Path validation reuses `file_path_is_safe()` verbatim -- the exact function `files[].path` already validates against, not a second set of rules. Response is raw bytes (`application/octet-stream`) via `http_write_response()` -- only the second non-JSON response body this daemon has ever produced, after the web dashboard's own static asset serving.

CLI: `kanxeoctl files get NAME --path=/some/path [--output=PATH]` (stdout if `--output=` omitted). Needed no new HTTP client primitive -- `kx_client_request()` already captures a response's raw bytes into `struct kx_response.body`/`body_len` regardless of Content-Type, reused as-is rather than adding a redundant `..._raw()` sibling.

One real bug caught only by running the daemon, not by review: the route-dispatch suffix check for `/files` initially used `strcmp()` against the tail of the parsed container name -- but that name still carries its own `?path=...` query string attached (nothing else on this route ever needed to), so a full `strcmp()` against the literal string `"/files"` never matched anything with a trailing `?...`, and every request 404'd by falling through to the generic get-one handler. Fixed with `strncmp()` against just the 6 characters that matter, leaving the query string for `url_query_param()` (called separately, against the original unstripped path) to parse.

`test/test_container_files.c` extended (previously write-path-only, confirmed by a full read before touching it): read-while-running with an exact byte-for-byte content check, read-after-exit via the upperdir fallback, read-after-exit via the image-rootfs fallback (reading `/bin/daemon_child` itself, guaranteed present in the fixture image and never staged via `files[]`), 400 on a traversal path and a non-`/`-leading path, 404 for an unknown container and for a real container with a missing path. 3 consecutive clean runs. Full clean rebuild (zero warnings) and the full 18-test daemon-linked regression sweep, all passing, no regressions from the shared `main.c` route-dispatch changes. Also verified through the real, compiled `kanxeoctl` binary end-to-end (not just the test harness's own HTTP client) against a real, freshly started daemon and a real running container -- `files get` in both stdout and `--output=` modes, and the 404 path, all confirmed against real bytes on disk.

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** no streaming/range request support (every file this daemon has reason to serve this way is small, no chunked-response precedent anywhere in this codebase); no directory listing (an operator wanting to browse a rootfs still needs `console` + `ls`); no new authentication/authorization boundary (same posture as every other endpoint here).

## Phase 40 part 2 (done): hostbuild artifact mechanism -- `pkg install`'s second mode, plus a real from-scratch Linux 6.18.40 kernel built end-to-end (ADR-0056)

Second part of the self-hosting plan: a way to build a standalone artifact (starting with the Linux kernel `kanxeod` boots) using Kanxeo's own container+recipe mechanism, without merging the output into any image's rootfs. `pkg_hostbuild_start()` (`daemon/src/pkg.c`) reuses the entire existing `pkg install` pipeline unmodified except at two points: the build container's lowerdir is a named, real image's own rootfs (`--build-image=`) instead of the always-shared toolchain sandbox, and completion harvests the output into `ARTIFACTS_DIR/<name>/` instead of merging it into any image. Storage reuses `pkg_find()`'s existing per-`(name, image)` slots via a reserved sentinel image name, `PKG_HOSTBUILD_IMAGE = "__hostbuild"` (soft-reserved by convention, same posture `PKG_BUILD_CONTAINER_NAME` already established) -- every existing state-machine path keeps working unmodified. A hostbuild recipe must have empty `pkg_depends` (dependency resolution targets "merge into an image," meaningless for a one-shot harvest; every prerequisite must already be in `build_image`'s own rootfs). New `POST /v1/pkg/hostbuild` and `GET /v1/pkg/hostbuild/{name}` (the latter a thin wrapper over the existing `pkg_get_one()`); new `kanxeoctl pkg hostbuild <name> --build-image=<image> [--wait] [--deploy]` (`--deploy`, for `name=="kernel"` specifically, reads the artifact and calls the existing `cmd_update()`).

One real, previously-latent bug fixed along the way: `merge_tree()`'s own `pkg_entry_add_file()` calls assumed a real manifest struct always existed; the hostbuild harvest path's `e = NULL` (no manifest -- nothing to ever `pkg delete`) would have crashed the daemon the moment any hostbuild's `$PKG_DESTDIR` actually got populated, latent only because every attempt up to that point had failed earlier in the pipeline. Fixed with a NULL guard at the top of `pkg_entry_add_file()` itself.

Real-world verification (a genuine, from-scratch Linux 6.18.40 kernel build against a real "dev" image, not a synthetic fixture) surfaced five further real, independent gaps, each found and fixed the same empirical way -- a real build failing at an exact point, never speculated at:

- **Fetch reliability**: this sandbox's own outbound network has real, reproducible mid-transfer connection resets on large downloads. `start_fetch_for()`'s curl invocation gained `--retry 8 --retry-all-errors --retry-delay 3 -C -` plus an `unlink()` of the destination immediately before each attempt -- confirmed empirically that plain `--retry` alone does *not* cover a raw connection reset (curl only auto-retries a curated transient-condition list), and that `-C -` without the `unlink()` is actively harmful across separate top-level attempts (resuming against an already-complete file from a prior attempt makes the server correctly answer HTTP 416, which every retry then hits identically).
- **`/bin/sh`**: this project's own images deliberately have no `/bin` at all -- fine for a minimal runtime container, a real gap for a build-toolchain image. The Linux kernel's own Kconfig `$(shell ...)` macro evaluation goes through glibc's `popen()`, which is POSIX-specified to always exec `/bin/sh` literally with **no override mechanism** (not `$SHELL`, not a `SHELL=` make variable). The failure did not surface as the expected "No such file or directory" -- it surfaced as a misleading `Cannot allocate memory`, traced through real memory/cgroup checks (all clear) and a throwaway diagnostic recipe (isolated it to GNU Make's own invocation specifically) before a direct `ls /bin/sh` confirmed the real cause. `bash.recipe` now installs a `/bin/sh -> /usr/bin/bash` symlink as part of its own install.
- **`-lpthread`**: glibc >= 2.34 folded pthread's functions into `libc.so.6` itself and ships no standalone `libpthread.so` at all anymore (confirmed via `dpkg -L libc6-dev` and a direct `find` on this exact host) -- but real build systems (the kernel's own `scripts/Makefile.host`) still pass `-lpthread` explicitly, which still resolves via the real, legitimate static `.a` compat archives glibc still ships. `libc-dev.recipe` never staged them; now it does.
- **`bison.recipe`**: a real, previously-undetected bug -- its `pkg_install()` stripped `usr/share` wholesale intending to trim docs, but took bison's own required *runtime* m4sugar/skeleton data down with it. Every earlier use of bison in this catalog happened to only ever run from inside the toolchain sandbox itself (still unstripped); this was the first time something ran bison from a *target* image's own copy.
- **Eight missing base tools**: `sed`, `grep`, `diffutils`, `bc`, `elfutils` (`libelf`, needed by `tools/objtool`), `zlib` (a real runtime dependency of `elfutils`' own `libelf.so`, never before staged into any target image), `findutils` (`xargs`), `gzip` (the kernel build's very last step, compressing `vmlinux`) -- each a genuine, real gap in a recipe catalog built up incrementally as real builds actually needed something, never speculatively pre-installed.

New `test_pkg.c` step 17 (automated, fast, no kernel build): a trivial hostbuild fixture proving a real artifact lands on disk at the documented path (and does *not* leak into any image's rootfs), `PKG_ERR_BUSY` is enforced symmetrically against a concurrent ordinary install, a non-empty `pkg_depends` is rejected with 400, and an unknown `build_image` is rejected with 404. 3 consecutive clean runs. The real kernel build itself (Linux 6.18.40, `x86_64`) was verified live against a real running daemon -- not part of the automated suite, since a real kernel compile is far too slow to run repeatedly -- producing a genuine, valid `bzImage` (confirmed via `file` and its boot-sector magic bytes `55 aa` at offset `0x1fe`, ~9.4MB).

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** Part 3 of the self-hosting plan (`tcc.recipe`, `kanxeo.recipe` self-building `kanxeod`/`kanxeoctl` from this repo's own self-hosted git remote, server-side `mkbootroot` assembly triggered by the daemon) is not started. Concurrent hostbuilds are out of scope (the existing v1 one-job-at-a-time invariant, unchanged).

## Phase 40 part 3 (done): the self-hosting plan closes -- `tcc.recipe`, `kanxeo.recipe`, server-side `mkbootroot` assembly (ADR-0057)

Third and final part: rebuilding `kanxeod`/`kanxeoctl`/`web` themselves, entirely from inside Kanxeo's own container+recipe mechanism, with TCC (this project's own Immutable Maxim -- never GCC) as the compiler doing the building.

`pkg/recipes/tcc.recipe`: an ordinary recipe (not a hostbuild itself) installing the real upstream tinycc 0.9.27 release onto a build image. Patches one real, confirmed upstream bug: `lib/bcheck.c` unconditionally references glibc's `__malloc_hook`/`__free_hook`, removed from `<malloc.h>` in glibc 2.34 (2021) -- four years after this TCC release. A `sed`-inserted glibc-version guard fixes it, the same way real downstream distro packages patch this exact issue.

`pkg/recipes/kanxeo.recipe`: `pkg_source` is Kanxeo's own self-hosted gitea archive-download endpoint (`https://git.home.arpa/api/v1/repos/itdlabs/kanxeo/archive/v1.4.0.tar.gz` -- gitea's REST API shape, not its documented web convention, which this instance's reverse proxy doesn't forward), pinned to a real annotated tag (`v1.4.0`) cut specifically for this snapshot -- no `git clone` inside the container is possible at all (the hostbuild container has no network, ADR-0056). Authentication needed a scoped, read-only gitea access token embedded in the URL, after discovering the org's own `visibility: limited` setting overrides an individual repo's public/private setting for anonymous access -- confirmed with the user via `AskUserQuestion` rather than widening the org's own access model. The committed recipe carries a placeholder (`REPLACE_WITH_REAL_TOKEN`), never a real secret, mirroring this project's own established pattern for the ISO installer's signing key.

`kanxeo.recipe` also builds+stages `build/mkbootroot` itself, alongside `kanxeod`/`kanxeoctl`/`web/` -- making each hostbuild round self-contained, since a freshly-deployed box has never had `mkbootroot` on it before (it was previously only ever a dev-machine build tool). `mkbootroot` itself runs server-side, inside `kanxeod` -- the API-First Mandate leaves no other option for the CLI. New `CONN_BOOTROOT_ASSEMBLE` conn kind (`daemon/src/main.c`) forks+execs+pidfd-tracks it exactly like the existing `CONN_PKG_FETCH` pattern, triggered from a new `pkg_build_completed()` out-parameter (`out_hostbuild_done_name`) that tells `main.c` which hostbuild job just completed, without `pkg.c` itself ever needing to know what any package name means. `kanxeoctl pkg hostbuild kanxeo --build-image=kanxeo-builder --wait --deploy` deliberately does not poll the async assembly step -- `--deploy` reads `kanxeod-root.squashfs` once the hostbuild job itself shows `installed`, and if the squashfs isn't there yet, `/system/update`'s own existing error handling for a missing `image_path` is the honest answer, not a second ad-hoc polling loop for one recipe name.

Real end-to-end verification surfaced two further genuine gaps in TCC's own build-time behavior, both root-caused against vanilla upstream tinycc source before being fixed, not guessed at: TCC maintains a separate, single-path search list for CRT startup objects (`crt1.o`/`crti.o`/`crtn.o`), defaulting to `/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu` -- distinct from its broader "libraries:" list, and distinct from GCC's own `LIBRARY_PATH` convention that `libc-dev.recipe` was staging those files for. `libc-dev.recipe` now stages a second copy of the six crt objects there. Separately, glibc >= 2.34's own `libc.so.6` carries a `DT_NEEDED` entry on `ld-linux-x86-64.so.2` itself, which TCC resolves through its general library-search list -- not the separate, narrower path the real dynamic loader lives at (`/lib64`). `pkg_seed_image_baseline()`'s `runtime_libs[]` table (`daemon/src/pkg.c`) now stages a second copy at `/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/` too. Both are additive, idempotent second copies of files already correctly staged for their original purpose -- nothing about the existing conventions was wrong, TCC simply has its own, narrower, genuinely different defaults.

`kanxeo-builder`'s minimal image was built up the same one-real-gap-at-a-time way every prior recipe catalog gap in this project has been: `tcc`+`make`+`libc-dev` first, then a real build failure surfaced a missing `/usr/bin/bash` (this project's images have no `/bin` at all), then a second failure surfaced missing `coreutils` (the root `Makefile`'s own `mkdir -p build`). This sandbox's own system CA trust bundle was also found stale relative to an individually-present cert (`git`'s GnuTLS-backed fetch worked, plain `curl`'s OpenSSL-backed one didn't) -- fixed, with the user's explicit approval, by appending the missing cert directly to the bundle.

Proven with one real, live, end-to-end operational round trip against a scratch, `--data-dir=`-isolated verify daemon (no automated test can safely exercise "rebuild the box running the test suite with itself"): `tcc hostbuild`, all five base-tool installs, then `pkg hostbuild kanxeo --build-image=kanxeo-builder --wait` fetched the real tagged, token-authenticated archive, built `kanxeod`/`kanxeoctl`/`mkbootroot` entirely with the just-installed TCC (confirmed via the daemon's own log showing the real `tcc -Wall -Werror ...` invocations, zero warnings) and reached `state=installed`; the daemon's own async assembly step then ran `mkbootroot` server-side and logged `kanxeo bootroot assembly: succeeded`; the resulting `kanxeod-root.squashfs` confirmed via `file` to be a genuine, valid squashfs image.

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** the final `kanxeoctl update --kernel=... --image=... && kanxeoctl reboot` deploy-and-reboot step, against a real box (not the scratch verify daemon), was not performed this phase -- the mechanism itself (identical to the already-proven `kernel` hostbuild's own `--deploy`) is unchanged and needs no new code, but an actual reboot onto self-built binaries is a live, consequential action deliberately left for an operator to trigger explicitly. `GET /v1/health` still has no build-identifying field (git tag/commit or build timestamp) -- flagged during planning as a real, small, but response-shape-touching gap, still open. GPU firmware re-staging on a kanxeo-only rebuild remains out of scope (a control-plane-only rebuild needs none). Concurrent hostbuilds remain out of scope, unchanged.

## Part 0.5 (done): host management networking unified into a real, API-managed network, plus a live-reconfigurable daemon port and an OpenSSL-backed HTTPS listener (ADR-0058, ADR-0059)

The first step of a larger bare-metal-readiness effort (kernel modules, disk quotas, CPU affinity/slicing, self-built ISOs -- a fresh kernel + installer ISO rebuilt and boot-tested first, confirming the existing pipeline still worked end to end before building on top of it). Real-world use of that ISO on the user's own test VM surfaced a genuine design gap, not a speculative one: `kanxeod`'s own management IP was set up by a one-shot, GRUB-boot-parameter-driven `rtnetlink` call straight against the physical NIC (`apply_static_ip()`), entirely outside the `network_def`/`POST /networks` model every container-facing network already used -- invisible to `GET /networks`, un-inspectable, un-repointable without a reinstall. The user pushed back directly, and this phase is the fix.

`kanxeo-install.c` gains `--interface=<name>`, threaded into `net.conf` alongside the existing `--ip=`/`--prefix=`/`--gateway=`. `main()` (not `boot_init()`, which stays purely about mounts) now bootstraps a reserved network named `mgmt` from those fields via the exact same mechanism every other network already uses -- `network_create()` + `network_attach_interface()` -- and derives `kanxeod`'s own bind address from `mgmt`'s gateway. `network_def` gains `is_management` (`network_set_management()`/`network_find_management()`); `network_delete()` and `network_detach_interface()` both unconditionally refuse (`409`) while it's set on the target network -- a new safety invariant this design introduces for the first time (previously no network could ever carry a host-owned address, so this footgun didn't exist).

A new, dedicated `GET`/`PUT /v1/system/daemon-config` resource owns the listen port, HTTP/HTTPS exposure, and the management-network repoint operation -- deliberately separate from generic network CRUD, since each has a real side effect (a live listen-socket rebind) plain network endpoints were never meant to trigger. Since `kanxeod` runs as real PID 1 on an installed system, every change here is a live, in-process socket operation (new socket bound + added to `epoll` before the old one is torn down, so a failure rolls back rather than ever leaving a gap), also persisted so it survives a real reboot.

HTTPS shipped as its own, deliberately separated follow-on once `daemon_config`'s port/network-repoint half was already solid (a persisted `https_enabled` toggle with nothing real behind it would have been exactly the kind of stop-gap the Immutable Maxims forbid): `libssl`/`libcrypto` linked directly into `kanxeod` (already load-bearing in this system via PKI's own subprocess use, confirmed to compile and link cleanly under TCC), a small additive `tlsconn.c` fd -> `SSL*` side table (rather than threading TLS-awareness through this daemon's ~100 request handlers, all of which only ever see a raw `fd`), and a second, independent listener reusing the already-issued PKI `"host"` leaf certificate. The non-blocking handshake is driven step-by-step from the existing single-threaded `epoll` loop (`SSL_ERROR_WANT_READ`/`WANT_WRITE` widening/narrowing `EPOLLOUT` interest as needed), with an explicit `SSL_pending()` re-check-and-loop after every read to handle OpenSSL's own internal buffering correctly.

Two real bugs were found and fixed via live testing against a running daemon, not guessed at: `g_tls_conns[]`'s BSS zero-init meant slot `.fd == 0` looked permanently "in use," so `tls_register()` silently registered nothing and TLS connections fell back to raw, undecrypted wire bytes -- fixed with an explicit `tls_init()` setting every slot to `-1`. `rebind_https_listener()` was missing the no-op short-circuit its HTTP counterpart already had, causing a spurious `EADDRINUSE` on any `daemon-config` PUT touching an unrelated field -- fixed with the same no-op check.

Verified: the full daemon-linked regression suite (24 binaries) green; a real manual end-to-end HTTPS check (`curl`/`openssl s_client` against the PKI host cert, live HTTP/HTTPS independent toggling); and the definitive `test_installer` QEMU run, including a second, fully independent reboot within the same test, confirming `mgmt`/`daemon_config`/TLS-listener state all persist correctly across a real reboot, not just within one boot session.

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** no CLI or web dashboard surface exists for `daemon-config` yet -- every other daemon capability in this project has shipped CLI+web alongside its REST endpoint, and this is a real, tracked inconsistency with that convention (not a violation of the API-First Mandate itself). A separate, previously reported bug -- the dashboard becoming unreachable on the user's test VM while ICMP ping kept working, leading hypothesis a stuck single-threaded `epoll` loop rather than a crash (PID 1 exiting would very likely have taken the network down too) -- remains open and unrelated to this phase's own changes; it needs the user to identify what dashboard action preceded the hang, or a look at the VM's own console, before it can be root-caused. Parts 1-5 of the bare-metal-readiness plan (`cpu.max` API wiring, `cpuset` CPU affinity, kernel module loading, disk quotas, ISO self-build) remain unstarted.

## Part 0.5 follow-up (done): `daemon-config` CLI + web dashboard

Closes the CLI/web gap Part 0.5 itself flagged above. `kanxeoctl daemon-config show`/`daemon-config set [--port=N] [--https-port=N] [--enable-http] [--disable-http] [--enable-https] [--disable-https] [--management-network=NAME]` (`cli/src/main.c`) mirrors `site show`/`site set`'s existing shape, except the PUT is a genuine server-side partial update (unlike `/system/site`, which requires all three fields together) -- the CLI sends only the fields actually given, no fetch-then-merge dance needed. Caught one real bug before it shipped: the `--management-network=` flag's `strncmp()` prefix length was off by one (counted 22 instead of the real 21), so the flag was never recognized at all -- found by actually running the command against a live daemon and getting "unknown option" instead of the expected 404 for a bogus network name, not by inspection alone. Fixed and re-verified, including a full live repoint to a real network confirming the daemon actually rebinds.

Web dashboard: a new "Daemon" leaf under System > Backup (alongside Site/Backup/Restore), `view-daemon-config` (`web/index.html`) with port/https-port/http-enabled/https-enabled/management-network fields, refreshed on the same poll-loop + dirty-flag pattern `refreshSiteConfig()` already established. The management-network `<select>` is populated from `GET /networks`, filtered to `has_gateway` networks (plus the currently-active one, even if that were somehow not true, so the form never silently shows an unselected value). Since this dashboard's own requests are relative to the page's own origin, changing the port or management network disconnects the page the instant it takes effect -- guarded with an explicit `confirm()` before submitting either, the same pattern the existing Reboot/Shutdown buttons already use for a different class of consequential action.

Verified: `kanxeoctl daemon-config show`/`set` against a real running daemon (including the `--management-network=` bug found and fixed above, and the boolean/error paths -- `--enable-https` with no PKI bootstrapped correctly surfaces the daemon's own 500); the dashboard's edited `index.html`/`app.js` confirmed served by a real daemon with the new markup/functions present, and `GET /v1/system/daemon-config` confirmed reachable underneath. **Not verified, per Zen:** no real browser click-through was possible in this sandboxed dev environment (no headless browser tooling available, same limitation noted in Phase 39's own write-up) -- verified instead via a clean JS syntax check (`node --check`), an HTML tag-balance check, and confirming the daemon serves the exact edited files. A genuine visual/interactive check is still owed the next time this runs somewhere a browser is actually available.

## Part 1 (done): `cpu.max` API/CLI wiring

Smallest part of the bare-metal-readiness plan -- the mechanism already existed end to end in the runtime library (`struct cgroup_limits.cpu_max`, written to the real `cpu.max` cgroup file by `src/cgroup.c`) but was hardcoded `NULL` in the daemon, with zero API/CLI surface. `create_container_from_body()` (`daemon/src/main.c`) now parses an optional `cpu_max` field exactly like `memory_max`/`pids_max` are already parsed -- a raw, cgroup-native `"<quota> <period>"` string (microseconds), passed straight through with no invented percentage/unit-conversion layer, the same pass-through convention `memory_max`'s bytes and `pids_max`'s raw count already use. `kanxeoctl run` gains `--cpu-max="QUOTA PERIOD"`, next to `--memory-max=`/`--pids-max=`. `docs/api/openapi.yaml`/`docs/api/README.md`/`docs/guides/cli-reference.md` document the new field/flag.

`test/test_container_lifecycle.c` gained a new step: creates a container with `cpu_max: "50000 100000"` over real HTTP, then reads `/sys/fs/cgroup/<name>/cpu.max` directly (the real, kernel-authoritative value, not just what the REST response claims -- this daemon doesn't echo `cpu_max`/`memory_max`/`pids_max` back at all) and confirms it matches exactly. Also manually verified through the real, compiled `kanxeoctl --cpu-max=` flag against a live daemon.

Full clean rebuild (zero warnings) and the fast daemon-linked regression set (23 binaries, excluding the QEMU-boot-based tests, which this narrow, install/boot-unrelated change has no reason to touch) all pass, 3 consecutive clean runs of the new test step.

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** Parts 2-5 (`cpuset` CPU affinity, kernel modules, disk quotas, ISO self-build) remain unstarted. `GET .../stats` (ADR-0054) still has no `cpu.max`-reporting field -- `cpu_max` is create-time-only, matching `memory_max`/`pids_max`'s own existing scope, not expanded here.

## Part 2 (done): CPU affinity (`cpuset`) (ADR-0060)

New mechanism this time, not just plumbing -- `cpuset.cpus` didn't exist anywhere in this codebase before. `struct cgroup_limits` gains `cpuset_cpus` (`include/container.h`); `cgroup_create()` (`src/cgroup.c`) writes it to the real `cpuset.cpus` cgroup v2 file when given, mirroring `cpu_max`'s own conditional-write shape exactly. A new `cgroup_enable_cpuset()` mirrors `cgroup_enable_io_accounting()` (ADR-0054) line for line -- best-effort, `+cpuset` written to the cgroup v2 root's own `cgroup.subtree_control`, called once at daemon startup alongside the existing io-accounting call, never fatal on failure. `image/kernel/qemu-part1.config` gains `CONFIG_CPUSETS=y` (a distinct controller from the `CONFIG_CGROUP_SCHED`/`CONFIG_FAIR_GROUP_SCHED` that already made `cpu.max` work -- that pair only covers bandwidth limiting, not which physical CPUs a cgroup's tasks may run on at all).

Daemon/API/CLI wiring mirrors Part 1's `cpu_max` exactly: `create_container_from_body()` parses an optional `cpuset_cpus` string field, raw `cpuset.cpus` range-list syntax (e.g. `"0-1,3"`), passed straight through with no reinterpretation; `kanxeoctl run` gains `--cpuset=0-1,3`, next to `--cpu-max=`. `docs/api/openapi.yaml`/`docs/api/README.md`/`docs/guides/cli-reference.md` document the new field/flag. ADR-0060 covers both Part 1 and Part 2 together, per the plan's own note that they're the same "expose an already-partially-built cgroup control" family of change.

`test/test_container_lifecycle.c`'s Part 1 helper (`read_cgroup_cpu_max()`) generalized into `read_cgroup_value(name, file, ...)`, reused for a new step: creates a container with `cpuset_cpus: "0"` over real HTTP, reads `/sys/fs/cgroup/<name>/cpuset.cpus` directly, confirms it matches exactly -- proven against this dev sandbox's own 4-CPU host, using `"0"` (not a range) specifically so it also passes on a single-vCPU test host. Also manually verified through the real, compiled `kanxeoctl --cpuset=` flag against a live daemon, including confirming `cpuset` actually appears in the root's own `cgroup.subtree_control` after daemon startup.

Full clean rebuild (zero warnings) and the fast 23-binary regression set all pass, 3 consecutive clean runs of the new test step.

**Not designed or built yet, per Zen:** the `CONFIG_CPUSETS=y` kernel config addition has not yet been verified by an actual kernel rebuild + boot test -- deliberately deferred and batched with Part 3's (kernel modules) and Part 4's (disk quota) own kernel config additions, per this plan's own kernel-config-consolidation decision, rather than three separate multi-hour rebuild cycles. This dev sandbox's own kernel (not the minimal Kanxeo-built one) already has the cpuset controller built in, which is what the live daemon-side verification above actually exercised -- the target/installed kernel's own support is the part still owed.

## Part 3 (done): kernel module loading (ADR-0061)

The largest, most novel part of the bare-metal-readiness plan by the plan's own admission: real target hardware this project has never seen before needs broader driver coverage than the fixed, hardwired QEMU/VirtIO set every prior phase has tested against. The central architectural constraint (ADR-0014, no initramfs -- the kernel mounts root directly, nothing can `modprobe` a driver before that) forced a real design decision the plan's original text didn't fully resolve: storage controllers that could plausibly hold root stay built directly into the kernel (`CONFIG_BLK_DEV_NVME=y`, `CONFIG_BLK_DEV_MD=y` + RAID levels, `CONFIG_SCSI=y` + `CONFIG_BLK_DEV_SD=y`, alongside the already-`=y` `CONFIG_SATA_AHCI`), while network and USB drivers -- never needed to reach root or console input -- become real loadable modules (`CONFIG_E1000E`, `CONFIG_IGB`, `CONFIG_IXGBE`, `CONFIG_R8169`, `CONFIG_TIGON3` [module `tg3.ko` -- a real Kconfig-symbol-vs-module-name mismatch, confirmed against the kernel source `Makefile`], `CONFIG_USB_EHCI_HCD`, `CONFIG_USB_STORAGE`). Broadcom `bnx2` deliberately excluded: it cannot function at all without a firmware blob loaded first, unlike the others here, which degrade gracefully -- staging `linux-firmware` blobs is real, separate, honestly-flagged future scope.

`kmod` (modprobe/depmod/insmod/lsmod/modinfo/rmmod) is real, unmodified upstream software (`pkg/recipes/kmod.recipe`, kernel.org's own 34.2 release), matching this project's own "reuse real software, never hand-roll dependency resolution" posture already established for bird/keepalived/coreutils/etc. `pkg/recipes/kernel.recipe` (the existing ADR-0056/ADR-0057 hostbuild recipe) is extended, not duplicated, to also build the real `.ko` tree (`make bzImage modules` -- see below for why this must be one invocation, not two) and run a real `depmod -b $PKG_DESTDIR "$(make -s kernelrelease)"`, explicitly version-pinned rather than defaulting to the build container's own running kernel. `image/src/mkbootroot.c` gains two new, optional (`""` = skip) trailing arguments, `modules-dir` and `kmod-bin-dir`, the same tolerant-default shape `firmware-dir` (ADR-0029) already established -- staged via a new `test_image_fixture_copy_dir_recursive()` (real `cp -a`, needed because a `.ko` tree nests) and the *existing* flat `copy_dir_files()` (its symlink-dereferencing `stat()` already turns kmod's six tool-name symlinks into independent, working copies with no new primitive needed). `kanxeod` gains `load_boot_modules()`, a curated, best-effort `modprobe` of the network/USB list above, called from `main()` -- deliberately *before* `bootstrap_management_network()`, since that function needs the kernel to have already detected the named physical interface, which only happens once its driver is loaded, and deliberately *not* from inside `boot_init()`, which stays purely about mounts per Part 0.5's own established boundary.
